An index formula for families of end-periodic Dirac operators
Alex R. Taylor

Abstract
We derive a transgression formula for the renormalized Chern character of the Bismut superconnection in the context of end-periodic fiber bundles and families of end-periodic Clifford modules. The transgression is expressed in terms of the Fourier-Laplace transform of the Bismut superconnection using the renormalized supertrace of Mrowka-Ruberman-Saveliev. Consequently, we establish an index formula for families of Dirac operators on end-periodic manifolds. The index formula involves a new “end-periodic eta form” which generalizes both the Bismut-Cheeger eta form and the end-periodic eta invariant of Mrowka-Ruberman-Saveliev.



1.  Introduction and main results

Given a closed Riemannian fiber bundle
Z\to B
, a Clifford module
E\to Z
, and a family of Dirac operators on the fibers, there is a natural class in the
K
-theory of the parameter space
B
called the
index bundle
which generalizes the Fredholm index of a single operator. The objective of a local index formula for families is to find a nice representative for the Chern character of the index bundle in de Rham cohomology. In 1985 Quillen
[
Qui85a
]
suggested an approach to this problem (later carried out by Bismut
[
Bis86
]
) in the category of closed manifolds using an infinite-dimensional,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded version of the transgression formula appearing in Chern-Weil theory, i.e., a formula for the variation of the Chern character of a path of connections, where one replaces connections with superconnections and the matrix exponential with the heat operator. One shows that

\frac{d}{dt}\Str\left(e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right)=-d_{B}\Str\left(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right)

and then integrating over
t\in(0,\infty)
obtains a representative in de Rham cohomology for the Chern character of the index bundle, that is

\ch(\nabla_{0})-\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))=-d_{B}\Str\left(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right).

In this formula,

1.

The local index form
\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))=\widehat{A}(T(Z/B))\wedge\ch^{\prime}(E)
is defined in terms of the local geometries of
Z
and
E
. Here
\widehat{A}(T(Z/B))
is the A-hat genus of the vertical tangent bundle and
\ch^{\prime}(E)
is the Chern character of the twisting curvature of
E\to Z
.

2.

The Chern character
\ch(\nabla_{0})
is a closed differential form on the base
B
whose cohomology class equals the Chern character of the index bundle, and the index formula gives an explicit description of this representative.

Of course, in order for this approach to succeed, one must construct a special superconnection
A_{t}
so that the short-time limit of
\Str(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})
exists. Bismut
[
Bis86
]
carried out this procedure via probabilistic methods to establish the families index formula in the case of closed manifolds. Berline and Vergne
[
BV87
]
and Donnelly
[
Don88
]
then gave proofs of the theorem based on the classical expansion of heat kernels (also see Berline, Getzler, Vergne
[
BGV92
, §10]
).

The local families index formula has seen generalizations to several categories of non-compact manifolds. Bismut and Cheeger
[
BC90a
,
BC90b
]
established a local index formula for families in the context of manifolds with boundary,

\ch(\nabla_{0})-\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}-d_{B}\Str\left(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right)

(1.1)

which generalizes the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index theorem
[
APS75
]
. Here the boundary contribution
\widehat{\eta}
is the Bismut-Cheeger eta form which generalizes the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer eta invariant. Melrose and Piazza
[
MP97
]
later proved the formula (
1.1
) in the more general context of manifolds with b-metrics, which includes the case of manifolds with cylindrical ends. Other examples include the index formulas of
[
TZ91
]
,
[
LM00
]
, and
[
AGR23
]
. The differential forms appearing in these index formulas often have interesting geometric interpretations, most notably the “anomaly formula” of Bismut-Freed for the curvature of the determinant line bundle associated with a family of Dirac operators, cf.
[
Qui85b
]
and
[
BGV92
, §10.6]
. Further, as Freed
[
Fre21
, §8]
explains, the index theory for families has many interesting connections with mathematical physics.

Another interesting non-compact setting is that of end-periodic manifolds, where the non-compact end is allowed to have some periodically repeating topology. Taubes
[
Tau87
]
originally introduced the index theory of elliptic operators on end-periodic manifolds to investigate exotic smooth structures on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and this end-periodic index theory has since found other interesting applications in the study of positive scalar curvature metrics and positive scalar curvature cobordism
[
MRS16
]
,
[
KRS19
]
,
[
HM20
]
and Seiberg-Witten invariants
[
MRS11
]
. The end-periodic eta invariant arising from the index formula
[
MRS16
, Theorem A]
has played a fundamental role in these applications.

Thus we are motivated by the variety of interesting applications surrounding on the one hand the index theory of end-periodic operators, and the index theory of families of Dirac operators on the other. The main result of this article is a families index formula in the category of manifolds with periodic ends – see Theorem
5.5
and Theorem
6.2
.

Theorem
.

Let
B
be a closed manifold. Let
M\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle with compact part
Z
, and periodic end modeled on an infinite cyclic covering
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
of an even-dimensional closed manifold
Y
. Included in the data of the periodic end is a function
f:\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
W_{0}=f^{-1}[0,1]
is the periodically repeating segment of the end.

Let
E\to M
be a family of end-periodic Clifford modules, with
D
the associated family of end-periodic Dirac operators, and suppose that the family
D
is Fredholm. Then the Chern character of the index bundle of
D
is represented in de Rham cohomology by the following differential form on
B
:

\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))-\int_{W_{0}/B}f\,\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Y))-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}.

(1.2)

Here,
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}
is a differential form on
B
whose degree 0 part is the end-periodic eta invariant of
[
MRS16
, Theorem A]
.

The approach taken by
[
MP97
]
in proving the index formula (
1.1
) is to derive a transgression formula for a renormalized supertrace adapted to the context of manifolds with cylindrical ends, namely the b-supertrace, and then apply the defect formula which describes the nonvanishing of the b-supertrace of a supercommutator. In proving the formula (
1.2
) it becomes apparent that many of the distinguishing features as compared with (
1.1
) originate from the presence of an additional term in the renormalized supertrace defect formula
[
MRS16
, Theorem 4.6]
in the end-periodic context. Most significantly, the end-periodic eta form
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}
in (
1.2
) involves a new term which arises in exactly this way. In §
5.2
we show that this new term can be interpreted geometrically in terms of the Hessian
\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df
; moreover, in §
5.3
we show that this Hessian measures the nontriviality of the periodic end in the sense that it vanishes if and only if the periodic end is actually cylindrical.

Acknowledgements.
I am deeply grateful to my advisor, Pierre Albin, for providing invaluable guidance on this project, and for patiently reviewing innumerable earlier drafts. I would also like to thank Nikolai Saveliev for kindly answering my questions via email regarding end-periodic index theory.

2.  End-periodic Riemannian fiber bundles

We start by recounting the basic components of end-periodic geometry. For a more detailed exposition we refer the reader to
[
Tau87
, §3]
and
[
MRS16
, §2]
. Let
Y
be any even-dimensional closed Riemannian manifold with nontrivial
H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
, called the
furled-up manifold
. We choose a primitive cohomology class in
H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
which specifies how to unfurl
Y
to produce the periodic segment. This is equivalent to choosing a smooth map
f_{0}:Y\to S^{1}
, in which case the cohomology class is given by
f_{0}^{*}[d\theta]\in H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
. Corresponding to this class, the infinite cyclic covering space
p:\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to Y
is defined via the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{1}.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{2\pi it}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

(2.1)

Thus
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}=\{(t,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times Y:e^{2\pi it}=f_{0}(y)\}
. We will denote the projection
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to\mathbb{R}
by
f(t,y)=t
. If we fix a basepoint
y_{0}\in Y
, then
f_{0}
is given by

\displaystyle f_{0}:Y\to S^{1}

\displaystyle f_{0}(y)=\exp\left(2\pi i\int_{\gamma(y)}f_{0}^{*}(d\theta)\right)

where
\gamma(y)
is any path starting at
y_{0}\in Y
and ending at
y\in Y
. For any other choice of path, the difference of the integrals over the two paths yields an integral of
f_{0}^{*}(d\theta)
over a closed loop based at
y_{0}
. This difference is necessarily an integer, so
f_{0}(y)\in S^{1}
is well-defined.

The infinite cyclic cover
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
will be the model for the end of an end-periodic manifold. To explain this, note that
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
can be constructed rather explicitly as follows. Suppose for simplicity that
Y
is connected. Define the periodic segment
W_{0}=f^{-1}[0,1]\subseteq\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
, which is a connected submanifold with two boundary components. Then
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
is diffeomorphic to the manifold obtained by recursively gluing copies of the segment
W_{0}
together along the boundary components:

\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\simeq\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}W_{k}=\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}\sqcup\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{-}

where each
W_{k}=f^{-1}[k,k+1]
is diffeomorphic to
W_{0}
, and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{\pm}
are the two ends of
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
. An
end-periodic manifold
with end modeled on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
is a smooth manifold
M
with a compact submanifold
Z\subseteq M
with boundary, such that
M\setminus Z
is diffeomorphic to
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}
. We let
\en(M)=M\setminus Z
denote the
periodic end
of
M
.

Remark 2.1
.

It is worth mentioning a few things at this point:

1.

In the special case where
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}=\mathbb{R}\times\partial Z
,
W_{0}=[0,1]\times\partial Z
,
Y=S^{1}\times\partial Z
, and
f(t,q)=t
, then
M
is just a manifold with a cylindrical end.

2.

By allowing
Y
to have multiple components we obtain the notion of an end-periodic manifold
M
with multiple periodic ends.

3.

The projection
f:\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to\mathbb{R}
in the diagram (
2.1
) has the property that
f(t+k,y)=f(t,y)+k
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and so the differential
df
descends to a well-defined 1-form on
Y
, which is exactly
f_{0}^{*}(d\theta)
. Following
[
MRS16
]
, the symbol
df
will everywhere denote this (not necessarily exact) 1-form on
Y
.

∎

A Riemannian metric
g_{Y}
on
Y
lifts to a metric
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}=p^{*}g_{Y}
on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
. A Riemannian metric
g
on
M
is called an
end-periodic metric
if its restriction to
\en(M)
coincides with
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}
for some
g_{Y}
on
Y
. In general we will always assume that an end-periodic manifold is equipped with an end-periodic metric.

Given any ordinary vector bundle
E_{Y}\to Y
we construct a lifted vector bundle
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}=p^{*}E_{Y}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
via the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

A vector bundle
E\to M
is called an
end-periodic vector bundle
if its restriction to
\en(M)
coincides with
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
for some
E_{Y}\to Y
. In the same vein, given a differential operator
P(Y)
acting on sections of
E_{Y}\to Y
, we can define a lifted differential operator
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}=p^{*}P(Y)
acting on sections of
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
. In particular we have

\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}(p^{*}u)=p^{*}(P(Y)u)

for any
u\in\Gamma(Y;E_{Y})
. A differential operator
P
acting on sections of an end-periodic vector bundle
E\to M
is called an
end-periodic differential operator
if its restriction to
\en(M)
coincides with
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}=p^{*}P(Y)
for some such differential operator
P(Y)
. We call
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}
the
normal operator
of
P
, also denoted by
N(P)=\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}
.

Remark 2.2

(Bounded geometry)
.

A smooth Riemannian manifold has bounded geometry if its injectivity radius is positive and all covariant derivatives of its curvature tensor are uniformly bounded. An end-periodic manifold
M
equipped with an end-periodic metric clearly has bounded geometry because the Riemannian metric on
\en(M)
is the restriction of the lifted metric
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{g}}=p^{*}g_{Y}
from
Y
and
p
is a local isometry. For the same reason, it is clear that an end-periodic vector bundle
E\to M
has bounded geometry.
∎

Remark 2.3

(Clifford modules)
.

Our notion of
Clifford module
coincides with the notion of Dirac bundle defined in
[
LM89
, Definition 5.2]
. The data of a Clifford module
E\to M
determines a distinguished Dirac-type operator
D:\Gamma(M;E)\to\Gamma(M;E)
given by composing the Clifford action with the Clifford connection, cf.
[
BGV92
, Proposition 3.42]
. In the presence of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading
E=E^{+}\oplus E^{-}
(compatible with the Clifford module data), the Dirac operator splits into the “chiral parts”
D^{\pm}:\Gamma(M;E^{\pm})\to\Gamma(M;E^{\mp})
. Whereas
D
is self-adjoint, the chiral operators satisfy
(D^{+})^{*}=D^{-}
. An important invariant associated with the Dirac operator is the
Fredholm index
,
\ind D^{+}=\dim\ker D^{+}-\dim\coker D^{+}
.
∎

Of course, the most important end-periodic differential operators for our purposes will be the end-periodic Dirac operators. Let
E_{Y}\to Y
be a Clifford module with associated Dirac operator
D(Y)
. The Clifford module structure on
E_{Y}\to Y
lifts to
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
, and the Dirac operator associated with this lifted structure is exactly the pullback
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}=p^{*}D(Y)
. Suppose that
M
is an end-periodic manifold with end modeled on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to Y
. An
end-periodic Clifford module
is by definition a Clifford module
E\to M
which is also an end-periodic vector bundle, with data coming from

1.

A Clifford module structure on
E|_{Z}\to Z
.

2.

A Clifford module structure on
E|_{\en(M)}\to\en(M)
which coincides with that of
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
on
\en(M)
(and glues together smoothly with the data on
E|_{Z}\to Z
).

Let
E\to M
be an end-periodic Clifford module and let
D=D(M)
be the associated Dirac operator. It follows from the definition of the Clifford module structure on
E\to M
that
D
coincides with
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}
along
\en(M)
, and so
D
is an end-periodic differential operator with normal operator
N(D)=\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}
. We call such an operator an
end-periodic Dirac operator
.

Remark 2.4
.

It is also natural to consider end-periodic differential operators of a slightly more general type, by requiring only that they be asymptotic to some periodic normal operator along the end, instead of coinciding exactly. Similarly, we could relax the definition of “end-periodic metric” by requiring it to be merely asymptotic to some periodic metric along the end. Such metrics have been considered for example in
[
Tau87
]
and
[
MPU96
]
. All of our results extend in a straightforward manner to these more general metrics and operators.
∎

Now we will introduce the setting in which our index formula for families of end-periodic Dirac operators takes place. A fiber bundle
M_{z}\relbar\mkern-9.0mu\relbar M\xrightarrow{\pi}B
of smooth manifolds is called an
end-periodic fiber bundle
if there is a fiber bundle
Y_{z}\relbar\mkern-9.0mu\relbar Y\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}B
such that

1.

M
is an end-periodic manifold with furled-up manifold
Y
.

2.

Each fiber
M_{z}
is an end-periodic manifold with furled-up manifold
Y_{z}
.

3.

The diffeomorphic identification of
\en(M)
with
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}
is compatible with each of the identifications of
\en(M_{z})
with
(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+})_{z}
in that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\en(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

We will also assume that the base
B
is closed. In this paper we will consider an end-periodic fiber bundle
M\xrightarrow{\pi}B
and a family of end-periodic vector bundles
E_{z}\to M_{z}
(which assemble into a total end-periodic vector bundle
E\to M
with
E|_{M_{z}}=E_{z}
). We will use the notation
\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi,\mathrm{ep}}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(M;E)
to denote the space of vertical families of end-periodic differential operators on
E\to M
, and
\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi_{1}}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(Y;E_{Y})
the space of vertical families of differential operators on
E_{Y}\to Y
.

For the fiber bundle
Y\to B
we adopt the geometric setup of
[
BGV92
, §10.1-10.3]
:

•

Choose a metric
g_{B}
on
TB
and a metric
g_{Y}
on
TY
. This choice of metric on
Y
induces a connection on the fiber bundle
Y\to B
, i.e., a splitting
TY=\pi^{*}TB\oplus T(Y/B)
by identifying
\pi^{*}TB
with the orthogonal complement of
T(Y/B)
. Let
g_{Y/B}
denote the induced metric on the vertical tangent bundle
T(Y/B)
and let
\pi_{V}
denote the projection onto the vertical component. Define the degenerate metric on
TY
by

g^{Y}_{0}(u,v)=g_{Y/B}(\pi_{V}u,\pi_{V}v).

(2.2)

We define the degenerate Clifford bundle
\Cl_{0}(Y)=\Cl(T^{*}Y,g^{Y}_{0})
with respect to this metric.

•

Suppose
E_{Y}
is a Clifford module for the vertical cotangent bundle of
Y
with Clifford action
\cl:\Cl(T^{*}(Y/B),g_{Y/B})\to\End E_{Y}
and Clifford connection
\nabla^{E_{Y}}
. From this Clifford module structure we get a vertical family of Dirac operators
D(Y)=(D(Y)^{z})_{z\in B}
.

•

Define the vector bundle
\mathbb{E}_{Y}=\pi^{*}\Lambda T^{*}B\otimes E_{Y}\to Y
and degenerate Clifford action
m_{0}:\Cl_{0}(Y)\to\End\mathbb{E}_{Y}
which acts on the differential form component of
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
via exterior multiplication and acts on the
E_{Y}
-valued component of
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
via the vertical Clifford action. More precisely, any
\alpha\in T^{*}Y
decomposes uniquely into horizontal and vertical parts
\alpha=\alpha_{H}+\alpha_{V}
, and then

m_{0}(\alpha)(\beta\otimes u)=(\alpha_{H}\wedge\beta)\otimes u+(-1)^{\deg\beta}\beta\otimes\cl(\alpha_{V})(u)

for any
\beta\otimes u\in\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y})
. The action then extends in an obvious way. Thus
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
is a Clifford module for the degenerate Clifford bundle
\Cl_{0}(Y)
. The Clifford module
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
is horizontally degenerate in the sense that
m_{0}(\alpha)^{2}=-g_{Y/B}(\alpha_{V},\alpha_{V})
, i.e.,
m_{0}(\alpha_{H})^{2}=0
.

•

With respect to the aforementioned degenerate Clifford module structure on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
, we define a Clifford connection on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
by

\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}=\pi^{*}\nabla^{B}\otimes 1+1\otimes\nabla^{{E}_{Y}}+\frac{1}{2}m_{0}(\omega)

where
\omega
is the 2-tensor on
Y
defined in
[
BGV92
, Definition 10.5]
in terms of the second fundamental form and the curvature associated with the vertical connection
\nabla^{Y/B}
and metric
g_{Y/B}
.

•

The Bismut superconnection
A(Y)
on
E_{Y}
is the Dirac operator on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
associated with (a) the degenerate Clifford action
m_{0}
and (b) the Clifford connection
\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}
on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
. Thus, for any orthonormal local frame
(e_{j})
for
TY
with dual coframe
(e^{j})
, we define

A(Y)=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}.

When restricted to
\Gamma(Y;E_{Y})
, the Bismut superconnection
A(Y)
can be decomposed as

A(Y)=D(Y)+A_{[1]}(Y)+A_{[2]}(Y)

where
A_{[j]}(Y):\Gamma(Y;E_{Y})\to\Gamma(Y;\pi^{*}\Lambda^{j}T^{*}B\otimes E_{Y})
for each
j\geq 0
. The rescaled Bismut superconnection is given by

A_{t}(Y)=t^{1/2}D(Y)+A_{[1]}(Y)+t^{-1/2}A_{[2]}(Y)

for any
t>0
. The Bismut superconnection
A(Y)
is a first-order differential operator which is odd with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
. See
[
BGV92
, Proposition 10.15]
for more details on the Bismut superconnection in the context of closed manifolds.

The following basic fact will enable us to transport the tools of end-periodic geometry to the family context.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
M\xrightarrow{\pi}B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle. For a family of end-periodic vector bundles
E\to M
with associated bundles
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\xrightarrow{p}Y
,
Y\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}B
and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}B
, and for any vector bundle
V\to B
, we have an isomorphism of vector bundles over
\en(M)
,

E\otimes\pi^{*}V|_{\en(M)}\simeq p^{*}(E_{Y}\otimes\pi_{1}^{*}V)|_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}}

where we identify
\en(M)\simeq\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}
. Thus
E\otimes\pi^{*}V\to M
is an end-periodic vector bundle. Similarly,
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}V\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
is an end-periodic vector bundle.

Proof.

Since
E
is an end-periodic vector bundle we have
E=p^{*}E_{Y}
over
\en(M)
. Moreover, from the compatibility condition (iii) in the definition of the end-periodic fiber bundle
M\to B
we have
\pi_{1}\circ p=\pi
after identifying
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}
with
\en(M)
, and therefore

p^{*}(E_{Y}\otimes\pi_{1}^{*}V)\simeq p^{*}E_{Y}\otimes p^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}V\simeq E\otimes\pi^{*}V

over
\en(M)\simeq\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}
. Owing to this isomorphism,
E\otimes\pi^{*}V
fits into the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\otimes\pi^{*}V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{Y}\otimes\pi_{1}^{*}V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\en(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{proj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

which is to say that
E\otimes\pi^{*}V
is an end-periodic vector bundle over
M
.
∎

Applying Proposition
2.5
with
V=\Lambda T^{*}B
we deduce that
\mathbb{E}=\pi^{*}\Lambda T^{*}B\otimes E\to M
and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}=\pi_{2}^{*}\Lambda T^{*}B\otimes\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
are end-periodic vector bundles. As a result, all of the geometric data (e.g., metrics, Clifford module structure, Clifford connections, etc.) of
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\to Y
lifts to
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
and then (together with the given data on
E|_{Z}\to Z
) yields end-periodic geometric data on
\mathbb{E}\to M
. We let
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}
denote the associated Bismut superconnection on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
and
A
the associated Bismut superconnection on
\mathbb{E}\to M
. We call
A
the
end-periodic Bismut superconnection
on
\mathbb{E}\to M
, it is an end-periodic first-order differential operator which is odd with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading on
\mathbb{E}
.

A key feature of the Bismut superconnection
A
is that it satisfies a generalized Lichnerowicz formula for the curvature
A^{2}
, see
[
BGV92
, Theorem 10.17]
and equation (9.25) of
[
MP97
]
. Let
L=(L^{z})_{z\in B}
denote the family of connection Laplacians associated with the connections
\nabla^{\mathbb{E},0}|_{M_{z}}
on the Clifford modules
E_{z}\to M_{z}
. Let
S_{M/B}
denote the scalar curvature of the fibers of
M\to B
, and let
F_{E}^{\prime}\in\Omega(M,\End_{\Cl(T^{*}(M/B))}(E))
denote the twisting curvature of
E
. Then

A^{2}=L+\frac{1}{4}S_{M/B}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a,b}m_{0}(e^{a})m_{0}(e^{b})F_{E}^{\prime}(e_{a},e_{b}).

(2.3)

where
(e^{a})
is an orthonormal local frame for
T^{*}M
. Similar formulas hold of course for the Bismut superconnections
A(Y)
on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\to Y
and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}
on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
.

Remark 2.6

(Supercommutator)
.

The natural Lie bracket in the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded setting is the supercommutator. For operators
P,Q
acting on sections of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded vector bundle
E=E^{+}\oplus E^{-}\to M
that are even or odd with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading, their supercommutator is defined as

[P,Q]=PQ-(-1)^{|P||Q|}QP

where we set
|P|=0
if
P
is an even operator and
|P|=1
if
P
is an odd operator.
∎

3.  Fourier-Laplace transform of the Bismut superconnection

One of the main tools we will use is the Fourier-Laplace (FL) transform introduced in the context of end-periodic geometry in
[
Tau87
, Proposition 4.2]
. Since
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
is an end-periodic vector bundle, we have for any
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
a well-defined FL transform, initially defined on compactly supported smooth sections by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\xi}:C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}})\to C^{\infty}(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y})

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\xi}u)(p(x))=\xi^{f(x)}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\xi^{m}u(x+m).

Here
x+m
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}
-action on the covering space
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
, i.e.,
(y,t)+m=(y,t+m)
, and the function
f:\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to\mathbb{R}
comes from the pullback diagram (
2.1
). Note that the right-hand side is a sum of vectors in the fiber
\mathbb{E}_{Y}|_{p(x)}
, because the fibers of
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}=p^{*}\mathbb{E}_{Y}
are by definition
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}|_{x+m}=\mathbb{E}_{Y}|_{p(x+m)}=\mathbb{E}_{Y}|_{p(x)}
. Also, the sum is finite because
u
is compactly supported.

For any vertical family of end-periodic differential operators
P\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi,\mathrm{ep}}^{s}(M;\mathbb{E})
, let
N(P)\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi_{2}}^{s}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}})
denote the normal operator. The FL transform extends by continuity to the Sobolev space
H^{k}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}},\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}})
for any
k\geq 0
, see

[
Ton23
, Proposition 3.15]
.
The FL transform defines a linear map

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}},\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}})\to\Rg\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{v:S^{1}\to C^{\infty}(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y})\}

by
(\mathcal{F}u)(\xi)=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}u
, which also extends by continuity to a linear isomorphism
\mathcal{F}:H^{k}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}L^{2}(S^{1},H^{k}(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y}))
, with inverse given by

\mathcal{F}^{-1}(v_{\theta})(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{S^{1}}\xi^{-f(x)}v_{\xi}(p(x))\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

(3.1)

Using the FL transform we may define another operator

FL(N(P)):L^{2}(S^{1},H^{k}(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y}))\to L^{2}(S^{1},H^{k-s}(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y}))

determined by the relation

\mathcal{F}\circ N(P)=FL(N(P))\circ\mathcal{F}.

Then for any
\xi\in S^{1}
, we get a vertical family of differential operators
P(Y)(\xi)\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi_{1}}^{s}(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y})
determined by the relation

\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\circ N(P)=P(Y)(\xi)\circ\mathcal{F}_{\xi}.

We call
P(Y)(\xi)
the
indicial family
of
P
. The operators
FL(N(P))
and
P(Y)(\xi)
are related by

FL(N(P))(\mathcal{F}u)(\xi)=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}(N(P)u)=P(Y)(\xi)(\mathcal{F}_{\xi}u)\in H^{k-s}(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y})

for any
\xi\in S^{1}
and
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}})
.

Taubes in
[
Tau87
]
used the FL transform to characterize the Fredholmness of end-periodic Dirac operators. In contrast with the situation for closed manifolds, an end-periodic Dirac operator
D
need not be Fredholm on
H^{1}(M;E)
; in addition to ellipticity,
D
must be “invertible at infinity”, a notion which is captured precisely by the invertibility of the indicial family
D(Y)(\xi)
. This is analogous to the case of manifolds with cylindrical ends, where a Dirac operator is Fredholm if and only if the Mellin transform of the indicial operator is invertible
[
Mel93
, Theorem 5.40]
. Throughout the paper we will use the following result of Taubes, which we have paraphrased from
[
Tau87
, Proposition 4.1, Lemma 4.3]
.

Theorem

(Taubes)
.

Let
E\to M
be an end-periodic Clifford module with end-periodic Dirac operator
D
and induced Dirac operators
D(Y)
and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}=N(D)
. The following are equivalent:

1.

D:H^{1}(M;E)\to L^{2}(M;E)
is Fredholm.

2.

\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}:H^{1}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}})\to L^{2}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}})
is invertible.

3.

D(Y)(\xi):H^{1}(Y;E_{Y})\to L^{2}(Y;E_{Y})
is invertible for every
\xi\in S^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

We note that the results of
[
Tau87
]
also show that the end-periodic Dirac operator can always be made Fredholm on a suitable
weighted
Sobolev space. Our results extend in a straightforward manner to the context of weighted Sobolev spaces, but for the sake of simplicity we will not include this detail. In the family context, Taubes’ theorem tells us that a family of end-periodic Dirac operators
D=(D^{z})_{z\in B}
is Fredholm on
H^{1}(M_{z};E_{z})
(i.e., the index
\ind D^{z}
is finite for every
z\in B
) if and only if the indicial families
D(Y)^{z}(\xi)
are invertible for every
z\in B
and
\xi\in S^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Throughout this paper we will be working under this assumption.

The following proposition summarizes some basic properties of the normal operator and the FL transform. The proof is a straightforward calculation.

Proposition 3.1

(Properties of the FL transform)
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi,\mathrm{ep}}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(M;\mathbb{E})
with associated differential operators
P(Y),Q(Y)\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi_{1}}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y})
and
N(P),N(Q)\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi_{2}}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}})
. Then

1.

N(PQ)=N(P)\circ N(Q)
.

2.

[PQ](Y)(\xi)=P(Y)(\xi)\circ Q(Y)(\xi)
for every
\xi\in S^{1}
.

3.

FL(N(P)N(Q))=FL(N(P))\circ FL(N(Q))
.

In the single-operator case (i.e., when the base
B
of the fiber bundle is a point), the indicial family of an end-periodic Dirac operator is

D(Y)(\xi)=D(Y)-\log(\xi)\cl(df),

(3.2)

for any
\xi\in S^{1}
, see equation (4.7) in
[
Tau87
]
, or equation (9) in
[
MRS16
]
. Note that
df
is a smooth 1-form on
Y
, cf. Remark
2.1
. There is a similar formula for the indicial family of the Bismut superconnection in the family case, which we will now derive.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
A
denote the Bismut superconnection on
\mathbb{E}\to M
and
A(Y)
the Bismut superconnection on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\to Y
. Thus we write

\displaystyle A=D+A_{[1]}+A_{[2]}

\displaystyle A(Y)=D(Y)+A_{[1]}(Y)+A_{[2]}(Y).

The indicial family of the Bismut superconnection
A
is given by

\displaystyle A(Y)(\xi)

\displaystyle=(D(Y)-\log(\xi)\cl(d_{Y/B}f))+(A_{[1]}(Y)-\log(\xi)\pi^{*}d_{B}f)+A_{[2]}(Y)

\displaystyle=D(Y)(\xi)+A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)+A_{[2]}(Y)

\displaystyle=A(Y)-\log(\xi)m_{0}(df).

Proof.

Since
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}=p^{*}E_{Y}
we have

\Gamma(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}})=\Gamma(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};p^{*}E_{Y})\simeq C^{\infty}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}})\otimes_{p^{*}C^{\infty}(Y)}p^{*}\Gamma(Y;E_{Y})

so any compactly supported section of
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}
has the form
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}=\varphi\cdot p^{*}u
for some
u\in\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y})
and
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}})
. By definition of the lifted Bismut superconnection
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}
we have
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}(p^{*}u)=p^{*}(A(Y)u)
and so

\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}=\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}(\varphi\cdot p^{*}u)=m_{0}(d\varphi)p^{*}u+\varphi\cdot p^{*}(A(Y)u),

noting that the principal symbol of
A(Y)
is the Clifford action
m_{0}
. Since
W_{0}
and
Y
are almost everywhere identified we will use the same symbol
x
to denote a point in
W_{0}
and its projection in
Y
. We fix
x\in Y
and calculate

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\circ\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}})(x)=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\bigg(m_{0}(d\varphi)p^{*}u+\varphi\cdot p^{*}(A(Y)u)\bigg)(x)

\displaystyle=\xi^{f(x)}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\xi^{k}\bigg(m_{0}(d\varphi|_{x+k})(p^{*}u)(x+k)+\varphi(x+k)p^{*}(A(Y)u)(x+k)\bigg).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle(A(Y)\circ\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}})(x)=A(Y)\cdot\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\xi^{f(x)}\xi^{k}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}(x+k)

\displaystyle=\log(\xi)m_{0}(df)(\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}})(x)+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\xi^{f(x)}\xi^{k}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}\cdot(\varphi(x+k)(p^{*}u)(x+k))

\displaystyle=\log(\xi)m_{0}(df)(\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}})(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt+\xi^{f(x)}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\xi^{k}\bigg(m_{0}(d\varphi|_{x+k})(p^{*}u)(x+k)+\varphi(x+k)p^{*}(A(Y)u)(x+k)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\log(\xi)m_{0}(df)(\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}})(x)+(\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\circ\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}})\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}(x).

where in the second line we make sense of the action of
A(Y)
on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}
using the fact that
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}|_{x+k}=E_{Y}|_{x}
, where the action coincides with that of
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}
. Comparing these two equations we see that

(\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\circ\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}})\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}=(A(Y)-\log(\xi)m_{0}(df))\cdot\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{u}}

which means that the indicial family is exactly
A(Y)(\xi)=A(Y)-\log(\xi)m_{0}(df)
.
∎

In the single operator case, the end-periodic eta invariant appearing in the index formula of Mrowka-Ruberman-Saveliev
[
MRS16
, Theorem A]
is expressed in terms of the indicial family of the end-periodic Dirac operator in question. The index formula states that the index of an end-periodic Dirac operator
D
is given by

\ind D^{+}=\int_{Z}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))-\int_{Y}f\,\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Y))-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mathrm{ep}}(D(Y)).

Here the end-periodic eta invariant
\eta_{\mathrm{ep}}(D(Y))
generalizes the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer (APS) eta invariant; it is defined in equation (27) of
[
MRS16
]
by the formula

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathrm{ep}}(D(Y))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\eta_{\mathrm{ep}}(D(Y))(t)\,dt

(3.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\oint_{S^{1}}\Tr\left(\cl(df)D_{\xi}^{+}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{-}D_{\xi}^{+}}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}\,dt

where
D_{\xi}=D(Y)(\xi)
is the indicial family from (
3.2
) associated with the end-periodic Dirac operator
D
. Whereas the APS eta invariant is a global spectral invariant of the boundary Dirac operator
D(\partial Z)
, the end-periodic eta invariant is a global spectral invariant of the entire indicial family. We will see that the indicial family of the end-periodic Bismut superconnection plays an analogous role in the index formula for families of end-periodic Dirac operators.

For a superconnection
A
on
\mathbb{E}\to M
, the action on
\Gamma(M;E)
decomposes into components

A=\sum_{j=0}^{\dim B}A_{[j]}

where
A_{[j]}:\Gamma(M;E)\to\Gamma(M;\pi^{*}\Lambda^{j}T^{*}B\otimes E)
. For
t>0
we define the rescaled superconnection

A_{t}=\sum_{j\geq 0}t^{(1-j)/2}A_{[j]}.

(3.4)

In general, if we have a linear operator
P
on
\mathbb{E}
which decomposes into components
P=\sum_{j}P_{[j]}
, then we will let
\delta_{t}P
denote the operator obtained by applying the same rescaling, namely,

\delta_{t}P=\sum_{j\geq 0}t^{(1-j)/2}P_{[j]}

so in particular
A_{t}=\delta_{t}A
. Another important example is the rescaled degenerate Clifford action

\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)=t^{1/2}\cl(d_{Y/B}f)+\pi^{*}d_{B}f,

as well as the rescaled version of the indicial family of the Bismut superconnection,

A_{t}(Y)(\xi)=A_{t}(Y)-\log(\xi)\delta_{t}m_{0}(df).

(3.5)

In order to prove the index formula we will need to be able to calculate the short-time limit of the pointwise supertrace of the heat kernel associated with the indicial operator
A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)
,

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)

for any
\xi\in S^{1}
. Unfortunately, the operator
A(Y)(\xi)
is not a Bismut superconnection with respect to the usual data on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
, so the result of
[
BGV92
, Theorem 10.23]
cannot be applied directly. However, we will show here that
A(Y)(\xi)
when twisted by a suitable connection produces a Bismut superconnection on a twisted bundle, which will enable us to calculate the short-time limit.

First we recall the notion of a first-order differential operator twisted by a connection, see for example
[
Pal65
, Chapter IV, §9, Theorem 3]
. Let
E\to M^{n}
be a vector bundle and let
D
be a first-order differential operator acting on sections of
E
. Let
\mathcal{W}\to M
be an auxiliary vector bundle with connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{W}}
and consider the twisted bundle
E\otimes\mathcal{W}\to M
. Then there is a unique first-order differential operator
D_{\mathcal{W}}
acting on sections of
E\otimes\mathcal{W}
satisfying
D_{\mathcal{W}}(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})=(Du_{1})\otimes u_{2}
whenever
\nabla^{\mathcal{W}}u_{2}=0
. The twisted operator can be locally constructed as follows. Let
\sigma_{1}(D)\in\Gamma(M;\Hom(T^{*}M,\End E))
denote the principal symbol of
D
, take a local frame
(e_{j})
for
TM
, and define

D_{\mathcal{W}}(u\otimes v)=(Du)\otimes v+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{1}(D)(e^{j})u_{1}\otimes\nabla^{\mathcal{W}}_{e_{j}}u_{2}.

Evidently this defines a first-order differential operator with the property that
D_{\mathcal{W}}(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})=(Du_{1})\otimes u_{2}
whenever
\nabla^{\mathcal{W}}u_{2}=0
. We will call
D_{\mathcal{W}}
the
twist of
D
by the connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{W}}
.

Now we will apply this twisting construction to show that the operator
A(Y)(\xi)
can be twisted to produce a Bismut superconnection on a twisted bundle. For any
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
let
L_{\xi}=\mathbb{C}\times Y\to Y
denote the trivial complex line bundle over
Y
equipped with the standard metric
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto z_{1}\overline{z_{2}}
on the
\mathbb{C}
fibers, and the connection

\nabla^{L_{\xi}}_{X}u=Xu-\ln\xi\cdot df(X)u

for any
X\in\Gamma(Y;TY)
and
u\in C^{\infty}(Y;\mathbb{C})
. Then
\nabla^{L_{\xi}}
is a flat connection on
\mathbb{C}\times Y\to Y
, and it is compatible with the aforementioned metric when
|\xi|=1
. We apply the twisting construction with
\mathcal{W}=L_{\xi}
and consider the twisted bundle
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}\to Y
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Consider the twisted bundle
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}\to Y
, equipped with the following structures:

•

The tensor product metric formed from the bundle metric on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
and the bundle metric on
L_{\xi}
.

•

The Clifford action
m_{0}\otimes 1
, acting on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
by
m_{0}
and acting on
L_{\xi}
by the identity.

•

The tensor product connection

\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi},0}=\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}\otimes 1+1\otimes\nabla^{L_{\xi}}.

Then we have the following:

1.

\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}\to Y
is a degenerate Clifford module with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}=(\mathbb{E}_{Y}^{+}\otimes L_{\xi})\oplus(\mathbb{E}_{Y}^{-}\otimes L_{\xi})
.

2.

The tensor product connection on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}
is a Clifford connection, and a metric connection when
|\xi|=1
.

3.

The Bismut superconnection
\mathbb{A}
on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}\to Y
