determined by the aforementioned data is precisely the twist of
A(Y)
by the connection
\nabla^{L_{\xi}}
on
L_{\xi}
, and
\mathbb{A}
is also the twist of
A(Y)(\xi)
by the trivial connection
d
on
L_{\xi}
.

The conclusion (a) is immediate. The conclusion (b) follows from a straightforward calculation using the fact that
\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}
is a Clifford connection. The proof of part (c) amounts to the following calculation. Let
\mathbb{A}
denote the Bismut superconnection on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}\to Y
. Take any local frame
(e_{j})
for
TM
, then the action of
\mathbb{A}
on any
u_{1}\otimes u_{2}\in\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi})
looks like

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})\nabla_{e_{j}}^{\otimes}(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})\left(\nabla_{e_{j}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}u_{1}\otimes u_{2}+u_{1}\otimes\nabla_{e_{j}}^{L_{\xi}}u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=A(Y)u_{1}\otimes u_{2}+\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})u_{1}\otimes\nabla^{L_{\xi}}_{e_{j}}u_{2}.

Note that the principal symbol of
A(Y)
as a first-order differential operator on
\mathbb{E}
is just the Clifford action
m_{0}
, and since
A(Y)(\xi)
and
A(Y)
differ by an order zero operator we also have
\sigma_{1}(A(Y)(\xi))=\sigma_{1}(A(Y))=m_{0}
. Thus the preceding calculation shows that
\mathbb{A}
is the twist of
A(Y)
by the flat connection
\nabla^{L_{\xi}}
on
L_{\xi}
. Going a step further, we also see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})

\displaystyle=A(Y)u_{1}\otimes u_{2}+\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})u_{1}\otimes\nabla^{L_{\xi}}_{e_{j}}u_{2}

\displaystyle=A(Y)u_{1}\otimes u_{2}+\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})u_{1}\otimes\left(e_{j}(u_{2})-\log(\xi)e_{j}(f)u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=A(Y)(\xi)u_{1}\otimes u_{2}+\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})u_{1}\otimes e_{j}(u_{2})

which shows that
\mathbb{A}
is the twist of
A(Y)(\xi)
by the trivial connection
d
on
L_{\xi}
.

4.  Renormalized supertrace

4.1. Asymptotically periodic smoothing operators

In order to make sense of the renormalized trace on end-periodic manifolds we first need a suitable class of operators to which it can be applied. In
[
MRS16
]
the authors show that their renormalized trace is well-defined on operators of the form
D^{m}e^{-tD^{2}}
for
m\geq 0
, which is all that is required to establish the index formula for end-periodic Dirac operators. Similarly, in our case too we will only need to apply the renormalized supertrace to certain smoothing operators. In this subsection we will define the notion of an asymptotically periodic smoothing operator. In the next subsection we will show that the renormalized supertrace is well-defined on these operators, and then show that the smoothing operators we are concerned with are asymptotically periodic.

Note that non-local operators constructed from end-periodic data do not necessarily have the property that they coincide with the induced normal operator along the end. For example, if
D
is an end-periodic Dirac operator on
E\to M
with normal operator
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}
, and
K
and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}
are the corresponding heat kernels, then
K
is in general asymptotic to
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}
along
\en(M)
(i.e.,
K
is not just the restriction of
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}
along the end), see
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.6]
. This leads to the notion of an asymptotically periodic smoothing operator, which we introduce below based on the heat kernel estimates of
[
MRS16
, §10]
.

Let
M^{n}\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle and
E\to M
a family of end-periodic vector bundles. Recall that we denote by
W_{0}
the “first copy” of the fundamental segment along
\en(M)
, i.e.,
W_{0}=f^{-1}[0,1]
. Given a vertical family of smoothing operators
H=(H^{z})_{z\in B}
on
\mathbb{E}\to M
we have a family of smooth integral kernels
K_{H}^{z}\in\Gamma(M_{z}\times M_{z},\Lambda T_{z}^{*}B\otimes E_{z}\boxtimes E_{z}^{*})
such that

(H^{z}u)(x)=\int_{M_{z}}K_{H}^{z}(x,x^{\prime})u(x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}

for any section
u\in\Gamma(M;E)
. For the sake of brevity we will often drop the
z
-superscript and write

(Hu)(x)=\int_{M/B}K_{H}(x,x^{\prime})u(x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime},

which one may interpret as a differential form on
B
given by
(Hu)(x):z\mapsto(H^{z}u)(x)
.

We say that
H
is
asymptotically periodic
if there exists a family of periodic smoothing operators
(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}^{z})_{z\in B}=(p^{*}H(Y)^{z})_{z\in B}
on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
with integral kernels
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}^{z}
such that the following estimates hold:

(AP1)

For any
z\in B
there exist constants
C,\gamma>0
such that

|K^{z}(x,y)|\leq Ce^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)}

for all
x,y\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}^{z}
, where
d(x,y)
is the Riemannian distance between
x
and
y
.

(AP2)

For any
z\in B
there exist constants
C,\gamma>0
such that

|K^{z}(x,x)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}^{z}(x,x)|\leq Ce^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,W_{0})}

for all
x\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}^{z}
.

(AP3)

For any
z\in B
there exist constants
C,\gamma>0
such that

|K^{z}(x,y)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}^{z}(x,y)|\leq Ce^{-\gamma d^{2}}

for all
x,y\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}^{z}
, where
d=\min\{d(x,W_{0}),d(y,W_{0})\}
.

The constants
C,\gamma
may depend on
z
but not on
x
or
y
. Also, they are not required to be the same for each estimate.

Note that an immediate consequence of (AP3) is that the operator
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}
to which
H
is asymptotic is uniquely determined, because if
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}_{1}
and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}_{2}
are two periodic operators satisfying (AP3) then their difference
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}_{1}-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}_{2}
must decay along
\en(M)
, but it is also periodic so it must be identically zero. Therefore, for an asymptotically periodic smoothing operator
H
we say that
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}=N(H)
is
the normal operator
of
H
.

Our principal objective in this section is to show that the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
associated with the end-periodic Bismut superconnection
A
is an asymptotically periodic smoothing operator, i.e., the heat kernel
K
satisfies the conditions (AP1), (AP2), (AP3) where
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}
is the heat kernel for the normal operator
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}
. Our proofs will for the most part follow those of
[
MRS16
, §10]
, except of course we will work with the Bismut superconnection
A
and families of heat kernels.

Let
A
denote the end-periodic Bismut superconnection on
\mathbb{E}\to M
adapted to an end-periodic Dirac operator
D
on
E\to M
. Consider the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
with smooth integral kernel
K(s,x,y)
. As a function of
s
and
x
with
y
fixed,
K
solves the initial value problem

\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+A^{2}\right)K(s,x,y)=0,\hskip 8.53581pt\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}K(s,x,y)=\delta_{y}\cdot\id

(4.1)

where
A
acts on the
x
variable and
\delta_{y}=\delta(x-y)
is the delta distribution supported at
y
. The following estimate, which is immediate from
[
Don88
]
, serves as the foundation for the heat kernel estimates we will establish in this section.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
M^{n}\to B
be a Riemannian fiber bundle with bounded geometry, and let
E\to M
be a Clifford module with bounded geometry. Let
A
denote the Bismut superconnection adapted to a family of Dirac operators
D=(D^{z})_{z\in B}
on
E
, and let
K^{z}(s,x,y)
denote the family of heat kernels. For any
z\in B
and any
T>0
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\left|\frac{\partial^{i}}{\partial s^{i}}\nabla_{x}^{j}\nabla_{y}^{k}K^{z}(s,x,y)\right|\leq Cs^{-n/2-i-|j|-|k|}e^{-d^{2}{(x,y)}/4s}

for all
s\in(0,T]
and
x,y\in M^{z}
, and the constant
C
depends only on
T
and
z
. Here
j
and
k
are multi-indices.

Proof.

This estimate follows from
[
Don88
, Proposition 4.1]
, which is stated in the context of families of Dirac operators and manifolds/vector bundles with bounded geometry. Also see
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.1]
where this estimate is stated for the case of a single Dirac operator.
∎

We will use the following lemma in the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold, and let
E
be a vector bundle with bundle metric
(\cdot,\cdot)
and metric connection
\nabla
. Let
L=\nabla^{*}\nabla
denote the connection Laplacian, and let
\Delta=d^{*}d=-*d*d
denote the scalar Laplacian acting on
C^{\infty}(M)
. Then for any section
u\in\Gamma(M;E)
we have

\Delta(|u|^{2})=2(Lu,u)-2|\nabla u|^{2}.

In particular, when
E=M\times\mathbb{R}
is trivial with flat metric and flat connection
d
acting on scalar functions, we obtain

\Delta(|u|^{2})=2u\Delta u-2|du|^{2}

for any
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

Proof.

Owing to the compatibility of the connection with the bundle metric we have

d(|u|^{2})=d(u,u)=2(\nabla u,u)\implies\Delta(|u|^{2})=d^{*}d(|u|^{2})=2d^{*}(\nabla u,u).

In an orthonormal local frame
(e_{j})
for
TM
write
(\nabla u,u)=\sum_{j}(\nabla_{e_{j}}u,u)e^{j}
. Then

\displaystyle d^{*}(\nabla u,u)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j}\nabla_{e_{j}}\cdot(\nabla_{e_{j}}u,u)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j}\left((\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}u,u)+(\nabla_{e_{j}}u,\nabla_{e_{j}}u)\right)

\displaystyle=(Lu,u)-|\nabla u|^{2}

and this completes the derivation.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
K(s,x,y)
denote the integral kernel for the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
. There exists constants
C,\alpha,\gamma>0
such that

|K^{z}(s,x,y)|\leq Ce^{\alpha s}s^{-n/2}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/s}

for all
x,y\in\en(M^{z})
and all
s>0
. In particular, for any
s>0
the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
satisfies (AP1).

Proof.

The estimate is obtained by following the proof of
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.2]
on each fiber, replacing the Dirac operator the Bismut superconnection
A
, and working with families of heat kernels. The same proof goes through with little change because the Bismut superconnection satisfies the Lichnerowicz formula
A^{2}=L+R
, cf. (
2.3
). Here
R
is given in terms of the scalar curvature of the fibers of
M\to B
and the twisting curvature of
E
, and
L=(L^{z})_{z\in B}
is the family of connection Laplacians on the fibers
E_{z}\to M_{z}
associated with the connections
\nabla^{\mathbb{E},0}|_{M_{z}}
.

We can apply Lemma
4.2
on each fiber
E_{z}\to M_{z}
with the scalar Laplacian
\Delta=d^{*}d
acting on smooth functions on
M_{z}
, and
u=K^{z}
the heat kernel along the fiber over
z\in B
(and similarly we can apply the scalar version of Lemma
4.2
with
u=|K^{z}|
on each fiber). We combine the resulting identities with Kato’s inequality

\big|d|K^{z}|\big|\leq|\nabla K^{z}|

(4.2)

which can be applied to a section of any Riemannian vector bundle with compatible connection, in particular our section
K^{z}
, see
[
MRS16
, Lemma 10.4]
.
This yields the differential inequality

\displaystyle|K^{z}|\frac{\partial}{\partial s}|K^{z}|+|K^{z}|\Delta(|K^{z}|)+(RK^{z},K^{z})

\displaystyle\leq 0

and therefore

\displaystyle|K^{z}|\frac{\partial}{\partial s}|K^{z}|+|K^{z}|\Delta(|K^{z}|)\leq-(RK^{z},K^{z})\leq\|R\||K^{z}|^{2}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{\partial}{\partial s}|K^{z}|+\Delta|K^{z}|\leq\|R\||K^{z}|\leq\alpha|K^{z}|

(4.3)

where we bound the curvature term
\|R\|\leq\alpha
using the bounded geometry of
M
. The rest of the proof then follows as in
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.2]
.
∎

The following lemma is the analogue in the family context of
[
Roe98
, Lemma 7.13]
which is used in the proof of
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.6]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
A
denote the Bismut superconnection on
\mathbb{E}\to M
. For any smooth function
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
and section
u\in\Gamma(M;\mathbb{E})
we have

A^{2}(hu)=m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}M}dh)u-2\nabla_{\grad h}^{\mathbb{E},0}u+hA^{2}u.

Proof.

Take any local orthonormal frame
(e_{j})
for
TM
. By definition we have

\displaystyle A(hu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 1}m_{0}(e^{j})\nabla_{e_{j}}^{\mathbb{E},0}(hu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 1}m_{0}(e^{j})(e_{j}(h)u+h\nabla_{e_{j}}^{\mathbb{E},0}u)

\displaystyle=m_{0}(dh)u+hAu.

Hence

\displaystyle A^{2}(hu)

\displaystyle=A(m_{0}(dh)u)+A(hAu)

\displaystyle=[A,m_{0}(dh)]u+hA^{2}u,

where the bracket denotes a supercommutator with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading on
\mathbb{E}
, cf. Remark
2.6
. Since
\nabla^{\mathbb{E},0}
is compatible with the Clifford action
m_{0}
, we have

[A,m_{0}(dh)]=m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}M}dh)-2\nabla^{\mathbb{E},0}_{\grad h}

and this completes the derivation of the formula. Note that the same calculation also works for the Bismut superconnection
A(Y)
on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\to Y
and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}
on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
K(s,x,y)
denote the integral kernel for the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
. There exists constants
C,\alpha,\gamma>0
such that

|\nabla^{\mathbb{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}},0}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}^{z}(s,x,y)|\leq Ce^{\alpha s}s^{-n/2-1}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,W_{0})/s}

for all
x,y\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}^{z}
and all
s>0
.

Proof.

The estimate follows by applying the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
4.3
to the section
\nabla^{\mathbb{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}},0}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}^{z}
. Also see the proof of
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.5]
where this estimate is stated for the case of a single Dirac operator.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
K(s,x,y)
denote the integral kernel for the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
, and let
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}(s,x,y)
denote the integral kernel for the heat operator
e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}^{2}}
. For any
z\in B
there exists constants
C,\alpha,\gamma>0
such that

|K^{z}(s,x,x)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}^{z}(s,x,x)|\leq Ce^{\alpha s}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,W_{0})/s}

for all
x\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}^{z}
and
s>0
. In particular, for any
s>0
the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
satisfies (AP2).

Proof.

The estimate follows by adapting the argument used in the proof of
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.6]
to the family setting: using property (AP1) and Lemma
4.5
, which we established above for the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
, together with Lemma
4.4
, which provides the family version of the curvature identity for the Dirac operator.
∎

Finally, we establish property (AP3) for the heat operator. The argument is similar to the proof of Proposition
4.6
for property (AP2).

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
K(s,x,y)
denote the integral kernel for the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
, and let
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}(s,x,y)
denote the integral kernel for the heat operator
e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}^{2}}
. For any
z\in B
and any given
T>0
there exists constants
C,\gamma>0
such that

|K^{z}(s,x,y)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}^{z}(s,x,y)|\leq Ce^{-\gamma d^{2}/s}

for all
0<s\leq T
and
x,y\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}^{z}
, where
d=\min\{d(x,W_{0}),d(y,W_{0})\}
. In particular, for any
s>0
the heat operator
e^{-sA^{2}}
satisfies (AP3).

Proof.

One may follow the proof of Proposition
4.6
, and in this case since
0<s\leq T
, replace the function
e^{\alpha s}
with a constant upper bound.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
A_{t}
denote the rescaled end-periodic Bismut superconnection, cf. equation (
3.4
). For any
s,t>0
, the heat operator
e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}
is an asymptotically periodic smoothing operator with periodic normal operator
e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
P\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi,\mathrm{ep}}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(M;\mathbb{E})
. Then
Pe^{-sA_{t}^{2}}
is also a vertical family of asymptotically periodic smoothing operators, with normal operator
N(P)e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}
.

In particular, it follows that for any
s,t>0
, the following smoothing operators are asymptotically periodic:

\mathrm{(i)}\ A_{t}e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}\hskip 8.53581pt\mathrm{(ii)}\ \frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2})e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}\hskip 8.53581pt\mathrm{(iii)}\ \mathcal{Q}=\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}.

Proof.

Write
H=e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}
with normal operator
N(H)=e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}
. It’s clear that the family
PH
consists of smoothing operators, because the algebra of smoothing operators is a two-sided ideal inside the algebra of bounded operators. The family of smoothing kernels for
PH
satisfy the estimates (AP1)-(AP3) with normal operator
N(PH)=N(P)e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}
because as we have shown above the heat kernels satisfy these estimates, and Proposition
4.1
yields estimates on the covariant derivatives of the heat kernels. For example, suppose for simplicity that
P
is a first-order differential operator, then locally we can write

P=\sum_{j}a_{j}\nabla^{\mathbb{E},0}_{X_{j}}

for some coefficient endomorphisms
a_{j}\in\Gamma(M;\End\mathbb{E})
and vector fields
X_{j}\in\Gamma(M;TM)
. Over the end of
M
we have
P=N(P)=p^{*}P(Y)
so the endomorphisms
a_{j}
are uniformly bounded. By Proposition
4.7
, for any
z\in B
we have constants
C,\gamma>0
such that

|K_{H}^{z}(x,y)-K_{N(H)}^{z}(x,y)|\leq Ce^{-\gamma d^{2}}

for all
x,y\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}^{z}
, with
d=\min\{d(x,W_{0}),d(y,W_{0})\}
. Using the estimate on covariant derivatives given by Proposition
4.1
and then following the argument of Proposition
4.7
again, we deduce that

|\nabla^{E}_{X_{j}}\cdot(K^{z}_{H}(x,y)-K^{z}_{N(H)}(x,y))|\leq C_{2}\exp(-\gamma_{2}d^{2})

for all
x,y\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}^{z}
and each
j
. Using this estimate together with the uniform boundedness of the coefficients of
P
we estimate

\displaystyle|K_{PH}^{z}(x,y)-K_{N(P)N(H)}^{z}(x,y)|

\displaystyle=|PK_{H}^{z}(x,y)-N(P)K_{N(H)}^{z}(x,y)|

\displaystyle=|PK_{H}^{z}(x,y)-PK_{N(H)}^{z}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j}|a_{j}^{z}(x)||\nabla^{E}_{X_{j}}\cdot(K^{z}_{H}(x,y)-K^{z}_{N(H)}(x,y))|

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\exp(-\gamma_{3}d^{2})

which is exactly the estimate (AP3). Similar arguments can be used to show that (AP1) and (AP2) hold as well.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
be asymptotically periodic smoothing operators. Then
N(H_{1}H_{2})=N(H_{1})N(H_{2})
.

Proof.

By definition, the normal operators are given by

N(H_{j})(p^{*}u)=p^{*}(H_{j}(Y)u),\ j=1,2

and so the proof amounts to a direct calculation as in Proposition
3.1
.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
A_{t}
denote the rescaled end-periodic Bismut superconnection. Consider the heat operator
H=e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}
which by Corollary
4.8
is asymptotically periodic with normal operator
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}=p^{*}(e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)})=e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}
. Let
H(Y)(\xi)
denote the indicial family of
H
. Then we have

H(Y)(\xi)=e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}.

Proof.

By definition, the heat operator
e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}
maps any
u\in\Gamma(Y;\mathbb{E}_{Y})
to a solution of the heat equation

\begin{cases}&\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)\right)e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}u=0\\
&\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}u=u.\end{cases}

We will show that
H(Y)(\xi)u
also solves this equation. Fix
\xi\in S^{1}
and suppose that
u=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}v
. Then

\displaystyle H(Y)(\xi)u=H(Y)(\xi)\mathcal{F}_{\xi}v=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\circ\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{H}}v=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\circ e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}v.

Furthermore

\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}\right)e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}v=0

and from Proposition
3.1
we know that the indicial family of the curvature
A_{t}^{2}
is
A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)=(A_{t}(Y)(\xi))^{2}
, so taking the FL transform yields

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathcal{F}_{\xi}+A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)\circ\mathcal{F}_{\xi}\right)e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}v=0

\displaystyle\implies\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)\right)H(Y)(\xi)u=0.

which is equation (
4.1
). Moreover,
H(Y)(\xi)u
satisfies the initial condition in equation (
4.1
). We have

\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}v=v

so taking the FL transform yields

\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}H(Y)(\xi)u=\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{F}_{\xi}e^{-s\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}v=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}v=u.

Thus
H(Y)(\xi)u
solves the heat equation (
4.1
) with the same initial condition. By uniqueness of solutions of the heat equation we deduce that
H(Y)(\xi)u=e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}u
for every
u\in\Rg\mathcal{F}_{\xi}
. Moreover the image of
\mathcal{F}
is dense cf.
[
Ton23
, Proposition 3.15]
, and the heat operators are continuous, so we conclude that
H(Y)(\xi)=e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}
as claimed.
∎

4.2. Renormalized supertrace defect formula

Let
X\to B
be a Riemannian fiber bundle with closed fibers and let
E\to X
be a family of vector bundles. Given a vertical family of smoothing operators
H=(H^{z})_{z\in B}
on
\mathbb{E}=E\otimes\pi^{*}\Lambda T^{*}B\to X
we have a family of smooth integral kernels
K_{H}^{z}\in\Gamma(X_{z}\times X_{z},\Lambda T_{z}^{*}B\otimes E_{z}\boxtimes E_{z}^{*})
such that

(H^{z}u)(x)=\int_{X_{z}}K_{H}^{z}(x,x^{\prime})u(x^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}.

for any section
u\in\Gamma(X;E)
. In particular, for any
z\in B
and
x\in X_{z}
we have
K_{H}^{z}(x,x)\in\Lambda T^{*}_{z}B\otimes(\End E_{z})|_{x}
so it makes sense to take the usual matrix trace of the endomorphism part. Thus the trace of
H
yields a differential form on
B
defined by fiber integrals of pointwise traces,

\displaystyle(\Tr H)(z)=\int_{X_{z}}\tr_{E}(K_{H}^{z}(x,x))\,dx

\displaystyle\mbox{ i.e., \ }\Tr H=\int_{X/B}\tr_{E}(K_{H}(x,x))\,dx.

Moreover, if
E\to X
is a family of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded vector bundles, then we get an induced
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading on
\mathbb{E}
given by
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}^{+}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{-}
with

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{+}=\left(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{\mathrm{ev}}T^{*}B\otimes E^{+}\right)\oplus\left(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}T^{*}B\otimes E^{-}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{-}=\left(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{\mathrm{ev}}T^{*}B\otimes E^{-}\right)\oplus\left(\pi^{*}\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}T^{*}B\otimes E^{+}\right).

With respect to this grading we define the supertrace of
H
by

\Str(H)=\Tr(H^{+})-\Tr(H^{-})

where
H^{\pm}
are the restrictions of
H
to
\mathbb{E}^{\pm}
. Thus the supertrace of
H
is a differential form on
B
given by

\displaystyle\Str(H):B\to\Lambda T^{*}B

\displaystyle\Str(H)(z)=\Str_{E|_{M_{z}}}(H_{z})=\Tr_{E|_{M_{z}}}(H_{z}^{+})-\Tr_{E|_{M_{z}}}(H_{z}^{-}).

See
[
BGV92
, pp. 309-314]
and
[
BGV92
, Lemma 9.14]
for reference.

In general when the fiber bundle
M\to B
is non-compact, the (super)trace of a smoothing operator may not be well-defined, and asymptotically periodic smoothing operators in particular need not be trace-class. In the context of end-periodic manifolds there is a coherent way of renormalizing the trace, see
[
MRS16
, §3.1]
where they define the end-periodic renormalized trace in the single-operator case. We will adapt this renormalized trace to the family context and show that it is well-defined on the space of asymptotically periodic smoothing operators.

Let
M\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle, and
E\to M
a family of end-periodic Clifford modules. For any vertical family of asymptotically periodic smoothing operators
H
we define the renormalized trace

\rTr H=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left[\int_{Z_{N}/B}\tr(K_{H}(x,x))\,dx-(N+1)\int_{W_{0}/B}\tr(K_{N(H)}(x,x))\,dx\right]

where
Z_{N}
is the submanifold of
M
composed of the compact part
Z
together with the first
N
segments of the end of
M
. The renormalized supertrace
\rStr H
is then obtained using the same formula but with the pointwise
\str
instead of
\tr
. Equivalently,
\rStr
is also given by

\rStr H=\rTr(H^{+})-\rTr(H^{-}).

The renormalized supertrace
\rStr
generalizes the
b
-supertrace of
[
MP97
]
equation (12.1) (also see
[
Mel93
, Definition 4.64]
). Much like other renormalized traces, the renormalized trace
\rTr
does not vanish on commutators; rather, we have a
trace defect formula
which expresses
\rTr[P,Q]
solely in terms of the indicial families of
P
and
Q
. The formula of
[
MRS16
, Theorem 4.6]
explains the renormalized trace defect for a single smoothing operator of the form
D^{m}e^{-tD^{2}}
where
D
is an end-periodic Dirac operator. Similarly, in the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded context, the renormalized supertrace
\rStr
does not vanish on supercommutators, but there is a supertrace defect formula that expresses
\rStr[P,Q]
in terms of indicial families. The
\bStr
defect for families of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded smoothing operators in the category of manifolds with cylindrical ends is explained by
[
MP97
, Proposition 9]
.

The renormalized (super)trace is well-defined for families of asymptotically periodic smoothing operators.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
M\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle and
E\to M
a family of end-periodic vector bundles. If
H
is a vertical family of asymptotically periodic smoothing operators on
E\to M
, then the limit as
N\to\infty
in the definition of
\rTr(H)
converges to a well-defined differential form
\rTr(H)\in\Omega^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(B)
.

Proof.

This follows just as in
[
MRS16
, Lemma 3.1]
using the fact that our asymptotically periodic operators satisfy the estimate (AP2) by definition.
∎

Recall that in the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded setting the bracket
[P,Q]
is a supercommutator, cf. Remark
2.6
. The following proposition explains the renormalized supertrace defect in the end-periodic
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded family context.

Proposition 4.13

(Supertrace defect formula)
.

Let
M\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle and
E\to M
a family of end-periodic vector bundles. Let
P
and
Q
be vertical families of asymptotically periodic smoothing operators. Then:

\displaystyle\rStr[P,Q]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{[P_{\xi},Q_{\xi}]}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial P_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}Q_{\xi}\right)d\xi

where the bracket is understood to be a supercommutator, i.e.,

\str\left(K_{[P_{\xi},Q_{\xi}]}\right)=\str\left(K_{P_{\xi}Q_{\xi}}-(-1)^{|P||Q|}K_{Q_{\xi}P_{\xi}}\right).

Proof.

Following the proof of the trace commutator formula
[
MRS16
, Theorem 4.6]
in the family context using the estimates (AP1), (AP2) and (AP3) with the ungraded chiral parts of
P
and
Q
, we deduce the
\rTr
defect formula in the family context,

\displaystyle\rTr[P^{-},Q^{+}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\tr\left(K_{P_{\xi}^{-}Q_{\xi}^{+}}(x,x)-K_{Q_{\xi}^{+}P_{\xi}^{-}}(x,x)\right)dx\,\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Tr\left(\frac{\partial P_{\xi}^{-}}{\partial\xi}Q_{\xi}^{+}\right)d\xi

and similarly for
\rTr[P^{+},Q^{-}]
.

Now our supertrace defect formula is proved by breaking the operators
P
and
Q
into their chiral parts, then applying the preceding formula to each part. If
P
and
Q
have opposite parities then
[P,Q]
is odd and both sides of the formula vanish, so it suffices to assume that
P
and
Q
are both even or both odd. If
P
and
Q
are both odd, we have

\rStr[P,Q]=\rTr[P^{-},Q^{+}]-\rTr[P^{+},Q^{-}]

and applying the family version of the
\rTr
defect formula to each term yields

\displaystyle\rStr[P,Q]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\tr(P_{\xi}^{-}Q_{\xi}^{+}(x,x)-Q_{\xi}^{+}P_{\xi}^{-}(x,x))\,dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Tr\left(\frac{\partial P_{\xi}^{-}}{\partial\xi}Q_{\xi}^{+}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\tr(P_{\xi}^{+}Q_{\xi}^{-}(x,x)-Q_{\xi}^{-}P_{\xi}^{+}(x,x))\,dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Tr\left(\frac{\partial P_{\xi}^{+}}{\partial\xi}Q_{\xi}^{-}\right)d\xi.

Note that

\displaystyle\str(P_{\xi}Q_{\xi})

\displaystyle=\tr(P_{\xi}^{-}Q_{\xi}^{+})-\tr(P_{\xi}^{+}Q_{\xi}^{-})

\displaystyle\str(Q_{\xi}P_{\xi})

\displaystyle=\tr(Q_{\xi}^{-}P_{\xi}^{+})-\tr(Q_{\xi}^{+}P_{\xi}^{-})

\displaystyle\Str\left(\frac{\partial P_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}Q_{\xi}\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr\left(\frac{\partial P_{\xi}^{-}}{\partial\xi}Q_{\xi}^{+}\right)-\Tr\left(\frac{\partial P_{\xi}^{+}}{\partial\xi}Q_{\xi}^{-}\right)

so grouping together terms in the preceding formula according to this pattern yields the statement of the proposition. The case where
P
and
Q
are both even operators is similar.
∎

In order to establish the transgression formula we will also need to apply the supertrace defect formula to supercommutators of the form
[A_{t},Q]
where
Q
is an asymptotically periodic smoothing operator. Since
A_{t}
is not a smoothing operator, this warrants some justification.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
A_{t}
be the rescaled end-periodic Bismut superconnection. The
\rStr
defect formula also holds in the case where
Q
is the asymptotically periodic smoothing operator
\mathcal{Q}=\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}
and
P=A_{t}
.

Proof.

For any
s>0
consider the smoothing operator
P_{s}=A_{t}e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}
, which is asymptotically periodic by Proposition
4.9
. We can apply the supertrace defect formula of Proposition
4.13
to obtain a defect formula for
\rStr[P_{s},Q]
. Since the integral kernels are continuous in
s
, and the limit as
N\to\infty
in the definition of
\rTr(P_{s}Q)
converges uniformly in
s
on bounded time intervals, the renormalized trace
\rTr(P_{s}Q)
is a continuous function of
s>0
. Similarly,
\rTr(QP_{s})
is continuous in
s
, hence
\rStr[P_{s},Q]
is also continuous in
s
. Therefore, we can interchange limits to calculate

\rStr[A_{t},Q]=\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\rStr[P_{s},Q]

which yields the desired result.
∎

4.3. Uniformity for the rescaled heat operator

In the single-operator case, the estimate of
[
MRS16
, Corollary 10.8]
ensures that the renormalized supertrace
\rStr(e^{-tD^{2}})
converges uniformly on bounded time intervals
t\in(0,T]
. This uniform convergence justifies interchanging limits to compute the short-time limit of
\rStr(e^{-tD^{2}})
in
[
MRS16
, Proposition 5.2]
. The analogue of
[
MRS16
, Corollary 10.8]
in the family context is Proposition
4.7
, which similarly guarantees uniform convergence of the renormalized supertrace
\rStr(e^{-sA^{2}})
for
s\in(0,T]
. However, when computing the short-time limit of the renormalized Chern character
\rch(A_{t})=\rStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})
, we fix
s=1
and let
t\to 0^{+}
. Unlike the single-operator case, where the exponent is simply
tD^{2}
, the curvature
A_{t}^{2}
of the rescaled Bismut superconnection includes additional
t
-dependent terms with distinct scaling.

In other words, although we have good control over the
s
-dependence in estimates for
e^{-sA^{2}}
, we require refined estimates for
e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}
that control the additional
t
-dependence. Crucially, we need these estimates to yield uniform convergence of
\rch(A_{t})
on bounded time intervals
t\in(0,T]
so that we can again justify interchanging limits when taking
t\to 0^{+}
to compute the short-time limit of
\rch(A_{t})
. We will establish this in Proposition
4.17
below. In §
6.1
we will use Proposition
4.17
to show that the short-time limit of
\rch(A_{t})
converges to the integral of a local index form.

Remark 4.15
.

Throughout this section we will use the notation

a(t)=O(t^{\infty})

to mean that
a(t)/t^{q}\to 0
as
t\to 0^{+}
for any
q>0
. We will also introduce the notation

a(t)\mathrel{\mathop{<}\limits_{\raisebox{1.80835pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}b(t)

to mean that
a(t)\leq C(t)b(t)
for some factor
C(t)>0
which converges to a finite value as
t\to 0^{+}
.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
M
be a manifold of bounded geometry, with injectivity radius
\iota=\mathrm{inj}(M)>0
. Let
B_{\iota}(m)
denote the geodesic ball of radius
\iota
centered at
m\in M
. Fix any
\gamma>0
. There exists constants
C,\beta>0
independent of
m\in M
such that, as
t\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{(i)}\hskip 8.53581pt\int_{B_{\iota}(m)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(m,z)/t}\,dz\leq C\left(\frac{t}{\gamma}\right)^{1/2}e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{4\gamma}}\int_{v(0)}^{v(\iota)}e^{-v^{2}}\,dv=O(t^{1/2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{(ii)}\hskip 8.53581pt\int_{M\setminus B_{\iota}(m)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(m,z)/t}\,dz\leq C\left(\frac{t}{\gamma}\right)^{1/2}e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{4\gamma}}\int_{v(\iota)}^{\infty}e^{-v^{2}}\,dv=O(t^{\infty})

where we define
v(r)=\left(\frac{\gamma}{t}\right)^{1/2}\left(r-\frac{\beta t}{2\gamma}\right)
. In particular this implies that

\int_{M}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(m,z)/t}\,dz\leq O(t^{1/2})

as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Proof.

Since
M
has bounded geometry, it has positive injectivity radius
\iota>0
. We decompose the integral over
M
into two parts,

\int_{M}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(m,z)/t}\,dz=\int_{B_{\iota}(m)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(m,z)/t}\,dz+\int_{M\setminus B_{\iota}(m)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(m,z)/t}\,dz.

We can use the coarea formula to calculate the integral over
M\setminus B_{\iota}(m)
as follows. Let
S_{r}(m)=\partial B_{r}(m)
denote the sphere of radius
r
centered at
m
inside
M
, which is the preimage of
r
under the Riemannian distance function
d(m,\cdot)
. Note that
d(m,\cdot)
is smooth almost everywhere, because it is smooth away from the cut locus of
m
, which has measure zero. Moreover, the rate of increase of distance along a geodesic is exactly
|\nabla d(m,z)|=1
. Therefore, by the coarea formula,

\displaystyle\int_{M\setminus B_{\iota}(m)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(m,z)/t}\,dz=\int_{\iota}^{\infty}\int_{S_{r}(m)}\frac{e^{-\gamma r^{2}/t}}{|\nabla d(m,z)|}\,d\sigma_{r}\,dr=\int_{\iota}^{\infty}\int_{S_{r}(m)}e^{-\gamma r^{2}/t}\,d\sigma_{r}\,dr

where
d\sigma_{r}
is the induced surface measure. The Bishop-Gromov inequality implies that geodesic balls have at most exponential surface area growth, i.e.,
\vol(S_{r}(m))\leq Ce^{\beta r}
for some constants
C,\beta>0
, therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\iota}^{\infty}\int_{S_{r}(m)}e^{-\gamma r^{2}/t}\,d\sigma_{r}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\iota}^{\infty}e^{-\gamma r^{2}/t}e^{\beta r}\,dr=Ce^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{4\gamma}}\int_{\iota}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\gamma}{t}\left(r-\frac{\beta t}{2\gamma}\right)^{2}}\,dr

where we complete the square in the exponent. Making the change of variables
v=\left(\frac{\gamma}{t}\right)^{1/2}\left(r-\frac{\beta t}{2\gamma}\right)
yields the bound (ii) in the statement of the proposition. Since
v(\iota)\to\infty
as
t\to 0^{+}
, the Gaussian integral over the tail
\{v\geq v(\iota)\}
decays like
O(t^{\infty})
as
t\to 0^{+}
by the well-known estimate

\int_{a}^{\infty}e^{-v^{2}}\,dv\leq\frac{e^{-a^{2}}}{a\sqrt{\pi}}

and so the upper bound decays like
O(t^{\infty})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

The integral over
B_{\iota}(m)
can be calculated in the same way, which yields the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\iota}(m)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(m,z)/t}\,dz\leq C\left(\frac{t}{\gamma}\right)^{1/2}e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{4\gamma}}\int_{v(0)}^{v(\iota)}e^{-v^{2}}\,dv=O(t^{1/2})

where
v(0)\to 0
and
v(\iota)\to\infty
as
t\to 0^{+}
. The Gaussian integral converges to
\sqrt{\pi}/2
, from which we obtain the
O(t^{1/2})
decay as
t\to 0^{+}
.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
A
be the end-periodic Bismut superconnection adapted to a family of end-periodic Dirac operators
D
, with normal operator
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}=p^{*}A(Y)
. Let
A_{t}
denote the rescaled Bismut superconnection. Then we have

|K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}}}(x,x)-K_{e^{-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}}(x,x)|\leq O(t^{\infty})

for all
x\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}
and
t\in(0,T]
. Consequently, the limit

\rch(A_{t})=\rStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})=\lim_{N\to\infty}s_{N}(t),

converges uniformly on bounded time intervals
t\in(0,T]
.

Proof.

First we explain how the convergence of the renormalized supertrace follows from the estimate in question. Indeed, the same procedure as in the proof of
[
MRS16
, Lemma 3.1]
shows how the convergence of the renormalized supertrace
\rStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})
translates to the convergence of the series

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{W_{0}/B}\tr(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}}}(x+k,x+k)-K_{e^{-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}}(x+k,x+k))\,dx.

The estimate in the statement of Proposition
4.17
implies in particular that this series converges uniformly on bounded time intervals
t\in(0,T]
.

The Lichnerowicz formula for the vertical family of Dirac operators (applied on each fiber) yields

D^{2}=(\nabla^{E})^{*}(\nabla^{E})+\frac{1}{4}S_{M/B}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\cl(e^{\alpha})\cl(e^{\beta})K_{E}^{\prime}(e_{\alpha},e_{\beta})

where
(e^{\alpha})
is a vertical orthonormal frame for
T^{*}(M/B)
. Comparing with the Lichnerowicz formula (
2.3
) for the Bismut superconnection, we see that the difference
R=A^{2}-D^{2}
is an endomorphism of
\mathbb{E}
given in terms of the curvature (and covariant derivatives of the curvature), and involves no derivatives. In particular, since
E
and
M
have bounded geometry, the components of
R
are all uniformly bounded. Rescaling via (
3.4
) yields
A_{t}^{2}=tD^{2}+t^{1/2}\delta_{t}R
, and we will write
R_{t}=t^{-1/2}\delta_{t}R
for brevity. The precise structure of the operator
R_{t}
is not important, the only thing we will use here is the fact that

|R_{t}|\leq r(t^{-1})=\sum_{j=1}^{4}c_{j}t^{-j/2}

(4.5)

for some constants
c_{j}>0
, using the uniform boundedness of
R
. According to the Volterra series for the heat operator, cf.
[
BGV92
]
equation (2.5), we have

e^{-A_{t}^{2}}=e^{-tD^{2}}+\sum_{m\geq 1}(-t)^{m}I_{m}

where
I_{m}
is the operator given by

I_{m}=\int_{\Delta^{m}}e^{-\sigma_{0}tD^{2}}R_{t}e^{-\sigma_{1}tD^{2}}R_{t}\cdots R_{t}e^{-\sigma_{m}tD^{2}}\,d\sigma

where
\Delta_{m}
is the standard
m
-simplex

\Delta_{m}=\{(\sigma_{0},\ldots,\sigma_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}:\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sigma_{j}=1,\sigma_{j}\geq 0\}.

Similarly, for the heat operator associated with the Bismut superconnection
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}
, we have

e^{-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}=e^{-t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}}+\sum_{m\geq 1}(-t)^{m}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}_{m}.

The sum over
m\geq 1
is finite because
I_{m}
is a sum of terms of degree at least
m
with respect to the grading of the differential forms on
B
. For the remainder of the proof we will fix the notation
\mathcal{K}(t,x,y)=K_{e^{-tD^{2}}}(x,y)
and
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}(t,x,y)=K_{e^{-t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}}}(x,y)
. We seek to estimate the difference of heat kernels

\displaystyle|K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}}}(x,x)-K_{e^{-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}}(x,x)|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{K}(t,x,x)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}(t,x,x)|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt+\sum_{m\geq 1}t^{m}|K_{I_{m}}(t,x,x)-K_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}_{m}}(t,x,x)|

(4.6)

for
x\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}
and
t\in(0,T]
. The estimate of
[
MRS16
, Corollary 10.8]
yields

|\mathcal{K}(t,x,x)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}(t,x,x)|\leq Ce^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,W_{0})/t}=O(t^{\infty})

(4.7)

for all
x\in\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}
and
t\in(0,T]
, so it remains to estimate the sum over
m\geq 1
. Consider the first term
m=1
. We can express the difference
I_{1}-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}_{1}
as a telescoping sum,

\displaystyle I_{1}-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\sigma tD^{2}}R_{t}e^{-(1-\sigma)tD^{2}}\,d\sigma-\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\sigma t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}_{t}e^{-(1-\sigma)t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(e^{-\sigma tD^{2}}-e^{-\sigma t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}})R_{t}e^{-(1-\sigma)tD^{2}}\,d\sigma+\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\sigma t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}}(R_{t}-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}_{t})e^{-(1-\sigma)tD^{2}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\sigma t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}_{t}(e^{-(1-\sigma)tD^{2}}-e^{-(1-\sigma)t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}})\,d\sigma

but
R_{t}=\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{R}}_{t}
along
\en(M)
because
A^{2}
is an end-periodic differential operator, so the middle term vanishes and we are left with

\displaystyle I_{1}-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(e^{-\sigma tD^{2}}-e^{-\sigma t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}})R_{t}e^{-(1-\sigma)tD^{2}}\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt+\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\sigma t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}}R_{t}(e^{-(1-\sigma)tD^{2}}-e^{-(1-\sigma)t\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{2}})\,d\sigma.

Hence the difference of integral kernels is bounded above by

\displaystyle|K_{I_{1}}(t,x,x)-K_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}_{1}}(t,x,x)|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt\leq\int_{0}^{1}\int_{M}|\mathcal{K}(\sigma t,x,y)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}(\sigma t,x,y)|\cdot|R_{t}\mathcal{K}((1-\sigma)t,y,x)|\,dy\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+\int_{0}^{1}\int_{M}|\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}(\sigma t,x,y)R_{t}|\cdot|\mathcal{K}((1-\sigma)t,y,x)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}((1-\sigma)t,y,x)|\,dy\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=(*)+(**).

Owing to the clear symmetry, the same argument can be used to estimate both terms. We focus on the first term
(*)
for the moment. Using the off-diagonal estimate
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.9]
we have

|\mathcal{K}(\sigma t,x,y)-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{K}}}(\sigma t,x,y)|\leq C_{1}e^{-\gamma d^{2}/\sigma t}\leq C_{1}e^{-\gamma d^{2}/t}

where
d=\min\{d(x,W_{0}),d(y,W_{0})\}
. Moreover, applying
[
MRS16
, Proposition 10.2]
together with the curvature bound (
4.5
), we have

\displaystyle|R_{t}\mathcal{K}((1-\sigma)t,y,x)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}r(t^{-1})e^{-\alpha(1-\sigma)t}t^{-n/2}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/(1-\sigma)t}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}r(t^{-1})e^{-\alpha(1-\sigma)t}t^{-n/2}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/t}

where
d(x,y)
is the Riemannian distance on
M
. Thus the term
(*)
is estimated by

\displaystyle(*)

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}r(t^{-1})t^{-n/2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{M}e^{-\gamma d^{2}/t}e^{-\alpha(1-\sigma)t}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/t}\,dy\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=C_{3}\left(\frac{1-e^{-\alpha t}}{\alpha t}\right)r(t^{-1})t^{-n/2}\int_{M}e^{-\gamma d^{2}/t}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/t}\,dy

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{<}\limits_{\raisebox{1.80835pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}r(t^{-1})t^{-n/2}\int_{M}e^{-\gamma d^{2}/t}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/t}\,dy

(4.8)

where we note that the factor
(1-e^{-\alpha t})/(\alpha t)
approaches
1
as
t\to 0^{+}
. In order to examine this integral over
M
, we split
M
into two submanifolds:

\displaystyle M_{0}(x)=\{y\in M:d(y,W_{0})\leq d(x,W_{0})\}

\displaystyle M_{1}(x)=\{y\in M:d(y,W_{0})\geq d(x,W_{0})\}

so that
d^{2}=d^{2}(y,W_{0})
on
M_{0}(x)
and
d^{2}=d^{2}(x,W_{0})
on
M_{1}(x)
. Then breaking (
4.8
) into two parts, we have

\displaystyle(*)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{<}\limits_{\raisebox{1.80835pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}r(t^{-1})t^{-n/2}\int_{M}e^{-\gamma d^{2}/t}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/t}\,dy

\displaystyle=r(t^{-1})t^{-n/2}\left(\int_{M_{0}(x)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(y,W_{0})/t}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/t}\,dy\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+r(t^{-1})t^{-n/2}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,W_{0})/t}\left(\int_{M_{1}(x)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/t}\,dy\right).

For
y\in M_{0}(x)
we have
d^{2}(y,W_{0})+d^{2}(x,y)\geq\frac{1}{2}d^{2}(x,W_{0})
by the triangle inequality, so we can estimate

\displaystyle\int_{M_{0}(x)}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(y,W_{0})/t}e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,y)/t}\,dy\leq\vol(M_{0}(x))e^{-\gamma d^{2}(x,W_{0})/2t}=O(t^{\infty}).

Lastly, using Lemma
4.16
we can bound the integral over
M_{1}(x)
by a function which decays like
O(t^{1/2})
as
t\to 0^{+}
. Putting all of this together yields

\displaystyle|K_{I_{1}}(t,x,x)-K_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}_{1}}(t,x,x)|

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{<}\limits_{\raisebox{1.80835pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}r(t^{-1})t^{-n/2}\cdot O(t^{\infty})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt+r(t^{-1})t^{-n/2}e^{-\gamma d(x,W_{0})/t}\cdot O(t^{1/2})

\displaystyle=O(t^{\infty})

because
r(t^{-1})
grows at most polynomially as
t\to 0^{+}
.

A similar argument holds for the remaining terms with
m>1
in (
4.6
), just with a longer telescoping sum and with more terms of the same type already discussed. Thus for every
m\geq 1
we have an estimate of the form

|K_{I_{m}}(t,x,x)-K_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{I}}_{m}}(t,x,x)|\mathrel{\mathop{<}\limits_{\raisebox{1.80835pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}p(t^{-1})\cdot O(t^{\infty})=O(t^{\infty})

where
p(t^{-1})
grows at most polynomially as
t\to 0^{+}
. Combining this estimate with (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) we obtain

|K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}}}(x,x)-K_{e^{-\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{A}}_{t}^{2}}}(x,x)|\leq O(t^{\infty})

as claimed.
∎

4.4. Special case: the b-supertrace defect

A particularly important case is that of manifolds with cylindrical ends, where the renormalized trace coincides with the
b
-trace defined in
[
Mel93
, §4.20]
. Melrose used the
b
-trace and its defect formula to give a proof of the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index theorem for manifolds with
b
-metrics, wherein the eta invariant arises naturally from the transgression formula for the variation of
\bStr(e^{-tD^{2}})
. In the setting of
families
of Dirac operators on manifolds with boundary, the analogous
b
-supertrace and its defect play a central role in
[
MP97
]
, where they are used to prove the index formula
[
MP97
, Theorem 1]
. There, the Bismut–Cheeger eta form emerges naturally from the transgression formula for the variation of
\bStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})
, with
A_{t}
the rescaled Bismut superconnection. In §
5.2
we will show that our end-periodic eta form reduces to the Bismut–Cheeger eta form in the cylindrical-end case. In this section we will introduce the
b
-trace and
b
-supertrace in preparation for that calculation.

Let
M\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle and
E\to M
a family of end-periodic Clifford modules. Suppose that the end of
M
is cylindrical, meaning
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}=\mathbb{R}\times\partial Z
,
Y=S^{1}\times\partial Z
, and
f(r,q)=r
. Recall that for any vertical family of asymptotically periodic smoothing operators
H
we defined the renormalized trace

\rTr H=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left[\int_{Z_{N}/B}\tr(K_{H}(x,x))\,dx-(N+1)\int_{W_{0}/B}\tr(K_{N(H)}(x,x))\,dx\right]

where
Z_{N}
is the submanifold of
M
composed of the compact part
Z
together with the first
N
segments of the end of
M
. In the case where the end of
M
is cylindrical, it is straightforward to see that the renormalized trace reduces to the
b
-trace
\bTr(H)
of
[
Mel93
, Definition 4.64]
and the renormalized supertrace reduces to the
b
-supertrace
\bStr(H)
of equation (12.1) of
[
MP97
]
. We will use the notation
\bTr(H)
when working on a manifold with cylindrical end.

As in the general end-periodic case, the
b
-trace defect
\bTr[P,Q]
is expressed in terms of the indicial families of the operators
P
and
Q
. The key difference in the cylindrical setting is that a slightly simpler indicial family can be used – one that arises from the Mellin transform of the normal operator instead of the Fourier-Laplace transform – and this is how the
\bTr
and
\bStr
defects are formulated in
[
Mel93
, Proposition 5.9]
and
[
MP97
, Proposition 9]
. The Mellin transform is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}:C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}})\to C^{\infty}(\partial Z;E_{\partial Z})

\displaystyle(\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}u)(q)=\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{-i\lambda}u(r,q)\,\frac{dr}{r}

for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, cf.
[
Mel93
, §5.1]
. This is just the Fourier transform after an exponential change of coordinates. Take any
P\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi,\mathrm{ep}}^{s}(M;\mathbb{E})
with normal operator
N(P)\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi}^{s}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}};\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathbb{E}}})
. For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
we define an operator
P(\partial Z)(\lambda)\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi}^{s}(\partial Z;\mathbb{E}_{\partial Z})
by the relation

\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}\circ N(P)=P(\partial Z)(\lambda)\circ\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}.

In
[
Mel93
]
the family of operators {
P(\partial Z)(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}
is called the
indicial family
of
P
. Thus we have so far defined two indicial families,
P_{\xi}(Y)=P(Y)(\xi)
and
P_{\lambda}(\partial Z)=P(\partial Z)(\lambda)
, obtained from the Fourier-Laplace transform and the Mellin transform respectively. When we want to distinguish the two we will say that
P_{\lambda}(\partial Z)
is the indicial family induced by the Mellin transform, and
P_{\xi}(Y)
is the indicial family induced by the Fourier-Laplace transform. The
b
-supertrace defect in the cylindrical end case is expressed in terms of the (Mellin transform induced) indicial family,

\bStr[P,Q]=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}[P_{\theta}(\partial Z)]Q_{\theta}(\partial Z)\right)d\theta,

(4.9)

cf.
[
MP97
, Proposition 9]
. The following proposition explains the relation between the indicial families induced by the Fourier-Laplace and Mellin transforms.

Proposition 4.18
.

For a family of end-periodic differential operators
P\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\pi,\mathrm{ep}}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(M;\mathbb{E})
with normal operator
N(P)=\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}
we have

K_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{S^{1}}\xi^{f(y)-f(x)}K_{P_{\xi}(Y)}(p(x),p(y))\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

(4.10)

for any
x,y\in\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
.

When
M
is a manifold with cylindrical end, i.e., with end modeled on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}=\mathbb{R}\times\partial Z
, in terms of the Mellin transform we have

K_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}}((r,q),(r^{\prime},q^{\prime}))=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathrm{Im}\,\theta=a}e^{i(r-r^{\prime})\theta}K_{P_{\theta}(\partial Z)}(q,q^{\prime})\,d\theta,

(4.11)

for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
as in the Mellin inversion theorem
[
Mel93
, Theorem 5.1]
. Moreover, in the cylindrical end case we have the identity

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{S^{1}}\Tr(P_{\xi}(Y))\frac{d\xi}{\xi}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr(P_{\theta}(\partial Z))\,d\theta.

(4.12)

Proof.

The formula (
4.10
) follows from the same direct calculation as in the single-operator case, cf.
[
MRS16
, Proposition 2.5]
. The formula (
4.11
) is derived from the same calculation again, but replacing the Fourier-Laplace transform with the Mellin transform. The identity (
4.12
) is then obtained by restricting the integral kernel formulas to the diagonal
x=y
and taking the trace.
∎

5.  Transgression formula and eta form

5.1. Transgression formula for periodic ends

In this section we derive a transgression formula for the renormalized Chern character of the rescaled end-periodic Bismut superconnection adapted to a family of end-periodic Dirac operators; i.e., a formula for the variation of the renormalized Chern character
\rch(A_{t})
.
