We first exhibit a broad outline of how the transgression formula is derived. By Duhamel’s formula we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\rStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})

\displaystyle=-\rStr\left[A_{t},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right]-\int_{0}^{1}\rStr\left[e^{-sA_{t}^{2}},\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2})e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}}\right]ds

\displaystyle=-\rStr\left[A_{t}-A_{[1]},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right]-\rStr\left[A_{[1]},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt-\int_{0}^{1}\rStr\left[e^{-sA_{t}^{2}},\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2})e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}}\right]ds.

Applying the
\rStr
defect formula to each of these three terms produces a total of six defect terms, all depending on the indicial family
A_{t}(Y)(\xi)
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\rStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})=(\alpha_{1}(t)-\alpha_{2}(t))+(\beta_{1}(t)-\beta_{2}(t))+(\gamma_{1}(t)-\gamma_{2}(t)).

Our derivation of the transgression formula will be completed by showing that

\displaystyle\mathrm{(i)}\hskip 8.53581pt

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(t)+\beta_{1}(t)+\gamma_{1}(t)=\frac{-1}{2\pi i}\frac{d}{dt}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)dx\,\frac{d\xi}{\xi},

\displaystyle\mathrm{(ii)}\hskip 8.53581pt

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}(t)+\gamma_{2}(t)=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t),\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle\mathrm{(iii)}\hskip 8.53581pt

\displaystyle\beta_{2}(t)=\mbox{exact form}.

Remark 5.1
.

In the case of manifolds with cylindrical ends, the transgression formula for the
b
-Chern character
\bch(A_{t})=\bStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})
has
\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}=\gamma_{1}=0
,
\gamma_{2}=0
, and the Bismut-Cheeger eta form is
\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}(t)=-\alpha_{2}(t)
, cf.
[
MP97
, Proposition 11]
.
∎

We start with the following calculation using Duhamel’s formula:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\rch(A_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rStr\left(e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}\rStr\left(e^{-sA_{t}^{2}}\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2})e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}}\right)ds

\displaystyle=-\rStr\left(\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2})e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right)-\int_{0}^{1}\rStr\left[e^{-sA_{t}^{2}},\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2})e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}}\right]ds

\displaystyle=-\rStr\left[A_{t},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right]-\gamma(t)

where we have introduced the shorthand

\gamma(t)=\int_{0}^{1}\rStr\left[e^{-sA_{t}^{2}},\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2})e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}}\right]ds

which is the correction that appears when we commute the two terms under
\rStr
. We will fix the following notation:

•

\mathcal{P}=A_{t}-A_{[1]}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\xi}=\mathcal{P}(Y)(\xi)
.

•

\mathcal{Q}=\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}=\mathcal{Q}(Y)(\xi)
.

•

\displaystyle H_{1}=e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}
and
\displaystyle H_{2}=\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi))e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}
.

Also note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(Y)(\xi)=A_{t}(Y)(\xi)-A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}(Y)(\xi)=\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}.

Returning to the time derivative of
\rch(A_{t})
and splitting
A_{t}=(A_{t}-A_{[1]})+A_{[1]}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\rch(A_{t})

\displaystyle=-\rStr\left[A_{t},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right]-\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=-\rStr\left[A_{t}-A_{[1]},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right]-\rStr\left[A_{[1]},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right]-\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=-\rStr[\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}]-\rStr[A_{[1]},\mathcal{Q}]-\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=-\alpha(t)-\beta(t)-\gamma(t)

(5.1)

where we introduce the abbreviations

\displaystyle\alpha(t)=\rStr[\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}]=\rStr\left[A_{t}-A_{[1]},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right],

\displaystyle\beta(t)=\rStr[A_{[1]},\mathcal{Q}]=\rStr\left[A_{[1]},\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}\right].

Let us focus on the first term
\alpha(t)
on the right-hand side of (
5.1
). By the supertrace defect formula we have

\displaystyle\alpha(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str(K_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}(x,x)+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}(x,x))\,dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\alpha_{1}(t)-\alpha_{2}(t).

(5.2)

The following lemma is the key step in understanding the term
\alpha_{1}(t)
.

Lemma 5.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\str(K_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{d}{dt}\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)-\str\left(K_{A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}}-K_{H_{2}H_{1}})\,ds

where we have set

H_{1}=e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\mbox{ \hskip 5.69054pt and \hskip 5.69054pt }H_{2}=\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi))e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}.

Proof.

By Duhamel’s formula we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\str\left(K_{\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}})\,ds

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{2}H_{1}})\,ds-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}}-K_{H_{2}H_{1}})\,ds

\displaystyle=-\str\left(K_{\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi))e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}}-K_{H_{2}H_{1}})\,ds.

The first term on the right-hand side here is

\displaystyle\str\left(K_{\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi))e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt=\str\left(K_{(A_{t}(Y)(\xi)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}+\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}A_{t}(Y)(\xi))e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt=\str\left(K_{((\mathcal{P}_{\xi}+A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi))\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}+\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}(\mathcal{P}_{\xi}+A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)))e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt=\str\left(K_{(\mathcal{P}_{\xi}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}+\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}\mathcal{P}_{\xi})e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)+\str\left(K_{[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}]e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}(1a)+(1b).

Thus

\frac{d}{dt}\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)=-(1a)-(1b)-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}}-K_{H_{2}H_{1}})\,ds.

Solving for (1a) yields

(1a)=-\frac{d}{dt}\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)-(1b)-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}}-K_{H_{2}H_{1}})\,ds.

On the other hand, the term
\alpha_{1}(t)
in equation (
5.2
) involves

\displaystyle\str(K_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}})

\displaystyle=\str\left(K_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}+K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}\right)

\displaystyle=\str\left(K_{(\mathcal{P}_{\xi}\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}+\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}\mathcal{P}_{\xi})e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)+\str\left(K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}[e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)},\mathcal{P}_{\xi}]}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}(1a)+(1c).

Then plugging in our previous expression for (1a) here we get

\str(K_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}})=-\frac{d}{dt}\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)-(1b)+(1c)-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}}-K_{H_{2}H_{1}})\,ds.

Now we look at the expression
-(1b)+(1c)
and use the following algebraic fact: if
A
is odd,
B
is odd, and
C
is even, then

[B,A]C=A[B,C]+[B,AC].

This identity yields

\displaystyle-(1b)

\displaystyle=-\str\left(K_{[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}]e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\str\left(K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}\left[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right]}\right)-\str\left(K_{\left[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right]}\right)

and therefore

\displaystyle-(1b)+(1c)

\displaystyle=-\str\left(K_{[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}]e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}\right)+\str\left(K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}[e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)},\mathcal{P}_{\xi}]}\right)

\displaystyle=-\str\left(K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}\left[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right]}\right)-\str\left(K_{\left[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right]}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+\str\left(K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}[e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)},\mathcal{P}_{\xi}]}\right)

\displaystyle=\str\left(K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}\left[e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)},A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\right]}\right)-\str\left(K_{\left[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right]}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+\str\left(K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}[e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)},\mathcal{P}_{\xi}]}\right),

where in the first term on the last line, we have changed the sign by swapping the operators inside the supercommutator (noting that
A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)
is odd and
e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}
is even). We can reunite the first and last terms here to obtain

-(1b)+(1c)=\str\left(K_{\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}[e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)},A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)+\mathcal{P}_{\xi}]}\right)-\str\left(K_{\left[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right]}\right),

but of course we have
A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)+\mathcal{P}_{\xi}=A_{t}(Y)(\xi)
by definition, so the first supercommutator on the right-hand side will vanish, and we are left with

-(1b)+(1c)=-\str\left(K_{\left[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right]}\right)=-\str\left(K_{A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)}\right),

which establishes the lemma.
∎

Returning to the term
\alpha_{1}(t)
and applying Lemma
5.2
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str(K_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}(x,x)+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}(x,x))\,dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\bigg[-\frac{d}{dt}\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\str\left(K_{A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}(x,x)+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)}(x,x)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\int_{0}^{1}\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}}(x,x)-K_{H_{2}H_{1}}(x,x))\,ds\bigg]\,dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

We compare this identity with

\displaystyle\beta(t)=\rStr[A_{[1]},\mathcal{Q}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}(x,x)+K_{\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\left(A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\right)\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\beta_{1}(t)-\beta_{2}(t),

and, noting that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are both even operators,

\displaystyle\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\rStr\left[e^{-sA_{t}^{2}},\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2})e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}}\right]ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str(K_{H_{1}H_{2}}(x,x)-K_{H_{2}H_{1}}(x,x))\,dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}ds

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial H_{1}}{\partial\xi}H_{2}\right)d\xi\,ds

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\gamma_{1}(t)-\gamma_{2}(t).

Thus, we see that

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{d}{dt}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76219pt-\beta_{1}(t)-\gamma_{1}(t).

Substituting this expression for
\alpha_{1}(t)
back into the transgression formula (
5.1
), cancellations occur and we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\rch(A_{t})

\displaystyle=-\alpha(t)-\beta(t)-\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{1}(t)+\alpha_{2}(t)-\beta_{1}(t)+\beta_{2}(t)-\gamma_{1}(t)+\gamma_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{d}{dt}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+\beta_{2}(t)+\alpha_{2}(t)+\gamma_{2}(t)

which in more detail is

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\rch(A_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{d}{dt}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\left(A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\right)\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial H_{1}}{\partial\xi}H_{2}\right)d\xi\,ds.

(5.3)

Looking at equation (
5.3
), our first term is a family version of the first term on the right-hand side of (23) in
[
MRS16
]
. We will argue that our second term
\beta_{2}(t)
is an exact form, and the last two terms together yield the end-periodic eta form.

Proposition 5.3
.

The term
\beta_{2}(t)
on the right-hand side of equation (
5.3
),

\beta_{2}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\left(A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\right)\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi\in\Omega^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(B)

is an exact form on
B
.

Proof.

Decompose the connection as
A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)=d+\varphi
where
\varphi
is a bundle map, and integrate by parts:

\displaystyle\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\left(A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\right)\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=-\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)\frac{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=-\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(d\cdot\frac{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\right)+\Str\left[\varphi,\frac{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\right]d\xi

\displaystyle=-d_{B}\left(\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\right)d\xi\right)

because
\Str\left[\varphi,\frac{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\right]=0
. For comparison see the analogous argument in
[
BGV92
, Lemma 9.15]
and the last line in the proof of
[
MP97
, Proposition 11]
.
∎

The proof of the transgression formula is completed by defining the time-dependent
end-periodic eta form
as the combination of the two remaining terms in equation (
5.3
) (with the opposite sign, to be consistent with
[
MP97
, Proposition 11]
). Thus we define the (time-dependent) end-periodic eta form by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{2}(t)-\gamma_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial H_{1}}{\partial\xi}H_{2}\right)d\xi\,ds.

In the next section we will clarify this object, and then state the end result in Theorem
5.5
. Depending upon the particular geometry of the fiber bundle
Y\to B
it is possible that
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
simplifies significantly; for example, we will show that when
Y=S^{1}\times\partial Z
is the furled-up manifold for a cylindrical end,
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
reduces to the time-dependent eta form of
[
MP97
, Proposition 11]
. The integral over
t\in(0,\infty)
of
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
yields the end-periodic eta form appearing in our index formula.

5.2. The end-periodic eta form

In this section we will present a geometric interpretation for the term
\gamma_{2}(t)
involved in the definition of the end-periodic eta form. We will also show that the latter reduces to the Bismut-Cheeger eta form when the periodic end is cylindrical, and that the degree zero component
(\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}})_{[0]}
is exactly the end-periodic eta invariant of
[
MRS16
]
.

We will abbreviate
A_{t}(Y)(\xi)=A_{t}(\xi)
. Recall the precise structure of indicial family of the rescaled end-periodic Bismut superconnection from equation (
3.5
). We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\xi}=A_{t}(\xi)-A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)=t^{1/2}D(Y)(\xi)-t^{-1/2}A_{[2]}(Y)

\displaystyle\implies\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(A_{t}(\xi)-A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi))=\frac{-t^{1/2}}{\xi}\cl(d_{Y/B}f).

Therefore the first term in the definition of
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
is

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(A_{t}(\xi)-A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi))\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(d_{Y/B}f)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

Note that this is very similar to the object in
[
MP97
]
equation (13.13) which, as
[
MP97
, Proposition 11]
argues in the cylindrical end context, is equal to the time-dependent Bismut-Cheeger eta form
\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}(t)
. We will show in this section that it does in fact generalize the time-dependent Bismut-Cheeger eta form. Now turning to the second term in the definition of
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
, we recall that we defined

\displaystyle 2\pi i\gamma_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial H_{1}}{\partial\xi}H_{2}\right)d\xi\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\left(e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\right)\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(\xi))e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\right)d\xi\,ds.

As we will explain, it is natural to think of this object in relation to the operator
[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]
where we set

\mathcal{B}(\xi)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}A_{t}(\xi)=-\xi^{-1}\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)

so that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(A^{2}_{t}(\xi))

\displaystyle=A_{t}(\xi)\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(A_{t}(\xi))+\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(A_{t}(\xi))A_{t}(\xi)

\displaystyle=[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]

\displaystyle=-\xi^{-1}[A_{t}(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)].

We will rewrite the second term
\gamma_{2}(t)
in the definition of
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
so as to exhibit its relation with the operator
[A_{t}(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]
. The following proposition explains the geometric significance of the operator
[A_{t}(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]
.

Proposition 5.4
.

With
A(Y)
the Bismut superconnection on the furled-up fiber bundle
Y\to B
, we have

[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]=m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)-2\nabla_{(d_{Y/B}f)^{\#}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}

(5.4)

and

[A(Y)(\xi),m_{0}(df)]=m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)-2\nabla_{(d_{Y/B}f)^{\#}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}+2\ln(\xi)|d_{Y/B}f|^{2},

where
(d_{Y/B}f)^{\#}
is the dual vector field with respect to the metric
g_{Y}^{0}
on
Y
, cf. (
2.2
). Moreover, if the periodic end is cylindrical, i.e.,
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}=\mathbb{R}\times\partial Z
and
f(r,q)=r
, then

m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)=\nabla_{(d_{Y/B}f)^{\#}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}=0

hence also
[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]=0
.

Proof.

We start by establishing the identity (
5.4
). The Bismut superconnection is by definition

A(Y)=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}

for any local orthonormal frame
(e_{j})
for
TY
. Note that

[\nabla_{e_{j}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0},m_{0}(df)]=m_{0}(\nabla_{e_{j}}^{T^{*}Y}df)

because
\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}
is a Clifford connection, i.e., compatible with the Clifford action
m_{0}
. Moreover, since
A(Y)
and
m_{0}(df)
are odd operators their supercommutator is an anticommutator. Therefore

\displaystyle[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]

\displaystyle=A(Y)m_{0}(df)+m_{0}(df)A(Y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}m_{0}(df)+m_{0}(df)m_{0}(e^{j})\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}m_{0}(df)+\left(-m_{0}(e^{j})m_{0}(df)-2g_{Y}^{0}(df,e^{j})\right)\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})\left(\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}m_{0}(df)-m_{0}(df)\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}\right)-2g_{Y}^{0}(df,e^{j})\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})[\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}},m_{0}(df)]-2g_{Y}^{0}(df,e^{j})\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}m_{0}(e^{j})m_{0}(\nabla_{e_{j}}^{T^{*}Y}df)-2g_{Y}^{0}(df,e^{j})\nabla^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}_{e_{j}}

\displaystyle=m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)-2\nabla_{(d_{Y/B}f)^{\#}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}.

Then the identity for
[A(Y)(\xi),m_{0}(df)]
is an immediate consequence of (
5.4
), because

\displaystyle[A(Y)(\xi),m_{0}(df)]

\displaystyle=[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]-2\ln(\xi)m_{0}(df)^{2}

\displaystyle=[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]+2\ln(\xi)|d_{Y/B}f|^{2}.

Suppose the periodic end is cylindrical. Then
f(r,q)=r
is just the lateral coordinate along the cylinder and
df
is a parallel covector field on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
, so the Hessian of
f
vanishes and
m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)\equiv 0
. Moreover,
\grad f=\partial_{r}
and by
[
MP97
]
equation (1.9) the connection on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}\to\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
is translation invariant, in other words we have
\nabla^{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{E}}}_{\partial_{r}}=p^{*}\nabla^{E_{Y}}_{\partial_{r}}=0
, hence

\displaystyle\nabla_{\partial_{r}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}\nabla^{T^{*}B}_{\partial_{r}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\nabla^{E_{Y}}_{\partial_{r}}+\frac{1}{2}m_{0}(\partial_{r}\lrcorner\,\omega)

\displaystyle=0+\frac{1}{2}m_{0}(\partial_{r}\lrcorner\,\omega).

Note that the left-hand side of this equation is a derivation (i.e. satisfies a product rule with respect to multiplication by scalar functions) whereas the right-hand side is
C^{\infty}(Y)
-linear, so we must have
\nabla_{\partial_{r}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}=0
. Thus
[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]=0
when the end is cylindrical.
∎

We will show later (cf. Proposition
5.8
) that the vanishing of the Hessian
m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)
is not just necessary but also sufficient to deduce that the end-periodic fiber bundle is cylindrical.

Now we will clarify the differential form
\gamma_{2}(t)
by rewriting it so as to exhibit its relation with the operator
[A_{t}(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]
. First, apply Duhamel’s formula to the
\xi
-derivative of the heat operator:

\displaystyle 2\pi i\gamma_{2}(t)=-\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{S^{1}}s\Str\bigg(e^{-suA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}

\displaystyle[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]e^{-s(1-u)A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(\xi))e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\bigg)\,d\xi\,ds\,du

which after cycling these even operators under the
\Str
becomes

\displaystyle 2\pi i\gamma_{2}(t)=-\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{S^{1}}s\Str\bigg(

\displaystyle e^{-s(1-u)A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(\xi))

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt\cdot e^{-(1-s(1-u))A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\bigg)\,d\xi\,ds\,du.

(5.5)

We introduce the shorthand

F(s(1-u))=\Str\bigg(e^{-s(1-u)A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(\xi))\cdot e^{-(1-s(1-u))A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\bigg).

Making the change of variables
v=s(1-u)
, the double integral (
5.5
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}sF(s(1-u))\,ds\,du=-\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}sF(v)\cdot\frac{-1}{s}\,dv\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}F(v)\,dv\,ds=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{v}^{1}F(v)\,ds\,dv=\int_{0}^{1}(1-v)F(v)\,dv.

Therefore

\displaystyle 2\pi i\gamma_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{S^{1}}sF(s(1-u))\,d\xi\,ds\,du

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{S^{1}}(1-v)F(v)\,d\xi\,dv

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{S^{1}}(v-1)\Str\left(e^{-vA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(\xi))e^{-(1-v)A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\right)d\xi\,dv

\displaystyle=\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\int_{0}^{1}(v-1)e^{-vA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(\xi))e^{-(1-v)A_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\,dv\cdot[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-uA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\,du\cdot[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{H}}_{t}(\xi)[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\right)d\xi

where we define the operator

\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)=\int_{0}^{1}e^{-uA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\,du.

Note that

[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]e^{-uA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}=[A_{t}(\xi)\mathcal{B}(\xi),e^{-uA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}]+[A_{t}(\xi)e^{-uA_{t}^{2}(\xi)},\mathcal{B}(\xi)]

for any
u>0
, hence also

[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)=[A_{t}(\xi)\mathcal{B}(\xi),\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)]+[A_{t}(\xi)\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]

and therefore

\Str\left([A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)=0.

Differentiating this latter equation with respect to
t
yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left([A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)d\xi+\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left([A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{\mathcal{H}}_{t}(\xi)\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)d\xi+2\pi i\gamma_{2}(t),

hence

\displaystyle 2\pi i\gamma_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(\xi),\mathcal{B}(\xi)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi},

and this completes our derivation of the transgression formula.

Theorem 5.5

(Transgression formula)
.

Let
M\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle and let
E\to M
be a family of end-periodic Clifford modules, with
D
the associated family of end-periodic Dirac operators. Let
A_{t}
be the rescaled end-periodic Bismut superconnection on
\mathbb{E}\to M
adapted to
D
. Then we have the following transgression formula for the renormalized Chern character:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\rch(A_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{d}{dt}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

(5.6)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)-d_{B}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\right)d\xi\right),

where the end-periodic eta form is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(d_{Y/B}f)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(Y)(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\frac{1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(Y)(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi},

with
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)=\int_{0}^{1}e^{-uA_{t}^{2}(\xi)}\,du.

Next we will show that the end-periodic eta form reduces to the Bismut-Cheeger eta form when the periodic end is cylindrical. In this case, the furled-up manifold is simply
Y=S^{1}\times\partial Z
with infinite cyclic cover
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}=\mathbb{R}\times\partial Z
. Our definition of
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
involves the
\gamma_{2}(t)
term which we initially defined as

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\left(e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi))e^{-(1-s)A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)d\xi\,ds.

In this integrand, the only contributing factors are those that produce a term which is
even
in
\xi
, because the terms which are
odd
in
\xi
will integrate to zero by symmetry. So we can simplify this expression by looking at the involved operators and identifying the parts that are even and odd in
\xi
.

Remark 5.6

(Even and odd functions in
\xi\in S^{1}
)
.

When we say that a function
h
on
S^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
is
odd
in
\xi
we mean that it satisfies
h(\overline{\xi})=-h(\xi)
for all
\xi\in S^{1}
. For example,
\xi\mapsto\ln(\xi)
is an odd function, whereas
\xi\mapsto\ln(\xi)^{2}
is even. A straightforward calculation shows that if
h
is odd in
\xi
then

\oint_{S^{1}}h(\xi)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}=0.\vskip-14.22636pt

∎

Denote the lateral coordinate along the cylindrical end by
f(r,q)=r
where
r\in[0,\infty)
. We also let
r
denote the periodic coordinate along the
S^{1}
-direction of
Y
. In this case the indicial family of the Bismut superconnection is

\displaystyle A_{t}(Y)(\xi)

\displaystyle=A_{t}(Y)-\ln(\xi)\delta_{t}m_{0}(dr)

\displaystyle=A_{t}(Y)-\ln(\xi)(t^{1/2}\cl(d_{Y/B}r)+\pi^{*}d_{B}r),

with curvature

\displaystyle A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)

\displaystyle=A_{t}^{2}(Y)-\ln(\xi)\delta_{t}[A(Y),m_{0}(dr)]-t\ln(\xi)^{2}|d_{Y/B}r|^{2}

\displaystyle=A_{t}^{2}(Y)-t\ln(\xi)^{2}

where we have used that
[A(Y),m_{0}(dr)]=0
in the cylindrical end case by Proposition
5.4
. Owing to this simplification, we have

e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}=e^{-t\ln(\xi)^{2}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)}=\mbox{even in }\,\xi.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)})=-2st\frac{\ln(\xi)}{\xi}e^{-st\ln(\xi)^{2}}e^{-sA_{t}^{2}(Y)}=\frac{1}{\xi}(\mbox{odd in }\,\xi),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi))=\frac{d}{dt}(A_{t}^{2}(Y))-\ln(\xi)^{2}=\mbox{even in }\,\xi.

Therefore

\gamma_{2}(t)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\mathrm{odd}\cdot\mathrm{even}\cdot\mathrm{even}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}\,ds=0

(5.7)

which is zero because the integrand is completely odd in
\xi
.

Since
\gamma_{2}(t)=0
in the cylindrical end case, the
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
form reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(dr)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(Y)(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

We introduce the shorthand
\mathcal{R}=\cl(dr)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}}
for the following calculation. Using Proposition
4.18
we calculate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{S^{1}}\Str(\mathcal{R}_{\xi}(Y))\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{neweqn})}}{{=}}\int_{W_{0}/B}\str(K_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{R}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{R}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{R}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{R}}}}(x,x))\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\partial Z/B}\str(K_{\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{R}}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{R}}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{R}}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{\mathcal{R}}}}((r,q),(r,q)))\,dq\,dr

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{neweqn2})}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\partial Z/B}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\str(K_{\mathcal{R}_{\theta}(\partial Z)}(q,q))\,d\theta\,dq\,dr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\partial Z/B}\str(K_{\mathcal{R}_{\theta}(\partial Z)}(q,q))\,dq\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Str(\mathcal{R}_{\theta}(\partial Z))\,d\theta.

Looking at the Mellin transform-induced indicial family with parameter
\theta
where
\xi=e^{i\theta}
, it is shown in
[
MP97
, Proposition 7]
and
[
MP97
, Proposition 11]
that

\displaystyle A_{t}(\partial Z)(\theta)=A_{t}(\partial Z)-i\theta m_{0}(dr)

\displaystyle A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)(\theta)=A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)+t\theta^{2}

\displaystyle e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)(\theta)}=e^{-t\theta^{2}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\theta}(\partial Z)

\displaystyle=\cl(dr)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}(\partial Z)(\theta)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)(\theta)}

\displaystyle=\cl(dr)\left[\frac{-i}{2}\theta t^{-1/2}\cl(dr)+\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}(\partial Z)\right]e^{-t\theta^{2}}e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)},

hence

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi i}\oint_{S^{1}}t^{1/2}\Str(\mathcal{R}_{\xi}(Y))\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Str(\mathcal{R}_{\theta}(\partial Z))\,d\theta

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{i}{2}\theta e^{-t\theta^{2}}\,d\theta\cdot\Str(e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)})

\displaystyle\hskip 34.1433pt-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-t\theta^{2}}\,d\theta\cdot t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(dr)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}(\partial Z)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)}\right)

\displaystyle=0-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Str\left(\cl(dr)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A_{t}}(\partial Z)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(\partial Z)}\right)

which is exactly the expression for the eta form in
[
MP97
]
equation (13.14).

Remark 5.7
.

As shown in
[
MRS16
, §6.3]
, the end-periodic eta invariant reduces to the classical APS eta invariant when the end is cylindrical. This is the degree zero part of what we have just shown above. The argument of
[
MRS16
]
relies on the fact that the eta invariant can be readily expressed in terms of the boundary spectrum, and calculations can be carried out using a complete orthonormal system of eigenvectors for the boundary Dirac operator. In contrast, our proof that the end-periodic eta form reduces to the Bismut–Cheeger eta form follows a rather different approach, because the eta form includes differential form components of higher degree that are not amenable to spectral methods.
∎

Now we will show that the degree zero component of the end-periodic eta form – the function
(\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}})_{[0]}\in C^{\infty}(B)
– is the fiberwise end-periodic eta invariant of
[
MRS16
, Theorem A]
. In particular this implies that the index formula of Theorem
5.5
reduces to the index formula of
[
MRS16
, Theorem A]
in the single operator case.

Considering a family of end-periodic Dirac operators
D=(D^{z})_{z\in B}
, the (time-dependent) end-periodic eta form in Theorem
5.5
is

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(d_{Y/B}f)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(Y)(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\frac{1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(Y)(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

By replacing the Bismut superconnection
A
with the Dirac opertor
D
, it is clear that the degree zero component of the first term is the fiberwise end-periodic eta invariant
z\mapsto\eta_{\mathrm{ep}}(D^{z}(Y_{z}))(t)
, cf. (
3.3
). It remains to show that the degree zero component of the second term is zero, i.e.,

\int_{0}^{1}\rStr\left[e^{-stD^{2}},D^{2}e^{-(1-s)tD^{2}}\right]ds=0.

This can be shown by the following direct calculation. We set
H_{1}=e^{-stD^{2}}
and
H_{2}=D^{2}e^{-(1-s)tD^{2}}
, and write
H_{j}(\xi)=H_{j}(Y)(\xi)
and
D_{\xi}=D(Y)(\xi)
for brevity. The supertrace defect formula yields

\displaystyle\rStr[H_{1},H_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{W_{0}}\oint_{S^{1}}f(x)\str(K_{H_{1}(\xi)H_{2}(\xi)}(x,x)-K_{H_{2}(\xi)H_{1}(\xi)}(x,x))\frac{d\xi}{\xi}\,dx

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(H_{1}(\xi))\cdot H_{2}(\xi)\right)d\xi.

Note that
H_{1}(\xi)H_{2}(\xi)=H_{2}(\xi)H_{1}(\xi)
, so the first term in
\rStr[H_{1},H_{2}]
vanishes. As for the second term in
\rStr[H_{1},H_{2}]
, we have

D_{\xi}^{2}=D^{2}-\ln(\xi)[D,\cl(d_{Y/B}f)]-\ln(\xi)^{2}|d_{Y/B}f|^{2}=D^{2}+\mathcal{C}(\xi)

where we write
\mathcal{C}(\xi)=-\ln(\xi)[D,\cl(d_{Y/B}f)]-\ln(\xi)^{2}|d_{Y/B}f|^{2}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(H_{1}(\xi))=-\int_{0}^{1}ste^{-ustD_{\xi}^{2}}\partial_{\xi}(\mathcal{C}(\xi))e^{-(1-u)stD_{\xi}^{2}}\,du

and

\displaystyle\oint_{S^{1}}

\displaystyle\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(H_{1}(\xi))\cdot H_{2}(\xi)\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{S^{1}}st\Str\left(e^{-ustD_{\xi}^{2}}\partial_{\xi}(\mathcal{C}(\xi))e^{-(1-u)stD_{\xi}^{2}}D_{\xi}^{2}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}}\right)d\xi\,du

\displaystyle=-st\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\partial_{\xi}(\mathcal{C}(\xi))D_{\xi}^{2}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}}\right)d\xi.

Note that

\partial_{\xi}(\mathcal{C}(\xi))=\frac{2}{\xi}(\cl(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)-\ln(\xi)|d_{Y/B}f|^{2})

and so

\displaystyle\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\partial_{\xi}(\mathcal{C}(\xi))D_{\xi}^{2}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=2\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\cl(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)D_{\xi}^{2}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt-2|d_{Y/B}f|^{2}\oint_{S^{1}}\Str(D_{\xi}^{2}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}})\ln(\xi)\,\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

Moreover we see that

\Str\left(\cl(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)D_{\xi}^{2}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}}\right)=0

because the operator inside the supertrace is odd with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading, and

\Str(D_{\xi}^{2}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}})=\frac{1}{2}\Str[D_{\xi},D_{\xi}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}}]=0.

In total this means

\oint_{S^{1}}\Str\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}(H_{1}(\xi))\cdot H_{2}(\xi)\right)d\xi=0

and hence that
\rStr[H_{1},H_{2}]=0
. Therefore the degree zero component of the (time-dependent) end-periodic eta form is

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)_{[0]}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(d_{Y/B}f)\frac{1}{2t^{1/2}}D_{\xi}e^{-tD_{\xi}^{2}}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Tr\left(\cl(d_{Y/B}f)D_{\xi}^{+}e^{-tD^{-}_{\xi}D_{\xi}^{+}}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

(5.8)

which is the function
z\mapsto\eta_{\mathrm{ep}}(D^{z}(Y_{z}))(t)\in\mathbb{R}
, mapping any
z\in B
to the scalar (time-dependent) end-periodic eta invariant of the fiber over
z
.

5.3. The Hessian of a periodic end

In §
5.2
we showed that the term
\gamma_{2}(t)
in the end-periodic eta form can be expressed in terms of the operator

[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]=m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)-2\nabla_{(d_{Y/B}f)^{\#}}^{\mathbb{E}_{Y},0}.

Moreover we showed in Proposition
5.4
that if
[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]
vanishes then the Hessian
m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)
vanishes, i.e.,
d_{Y/B}f
is a parallel 1-form along the fibers of
Y\to B
, which is reminiscent of the parallel 1-form
dr
along a cylindrical end. On the other hand, if
[A(Y),m_{0}(df)]
vanishes then the end-periodic eta form reduces to

\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)=\frac{-1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(dr)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(Y)(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

which is structurally similar to the Bismut-Cheeger eta form in the cylindrical end case. This leads to the question of whether the periodic end is actually cylindrical in this case. We will show that the periodic end is indeed cylindrical whenever
m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)\equiv 0
; in other words, when the periodic end is noncylindrical then
m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)\not\equiv 0
which suggests that the new term in the end-periodic eta form is in general nonzero.

We recall from §
2
the construction of the infinite cyclic covering space
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to Y
. Let
(Y,g_{Y})
be a smooth, closed, connected Riemannian manifold. One chooses a primitive cohomology class in
H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{Z})
, or equivalently a smooth map
f_{0}:Y\to S^{1}
. Corresponding to this class, the infinite cyclic covering space
p:\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to Y
is defined via the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{1}.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

From the pullback diagram we see that the differential
df\in\Omega^{1}(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}})
descends to the 1-form
f_{0}^{*}(d\theta)
on
Y
, in other words we have
df=p^{*}(f_{0}^{*}(d\theta))
. We lift the geometric data from
Y
up to
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
via pullback: the metric
g
on
TY
lifts to a metric on
T\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
, and the Levi-Civita connection on
T\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
with respect to the lifted metric is the lift of the Levi-Civita connection on
TY
.

Motivated by the remarks at the beginning of this subsection we will consider the special case where
df
is a parallel 1-form on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
, i.e.,
\nabla^{T^{*}\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}}df=0
with respect to the Levi-Civita connection on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
. Note that
p
is a Riemannian covering map when
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
is equipped with the pullback metric
p^{*}g_{Y}
, and so
df
is parallel on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
if and only if
f_{0}^{*}(d\theta)
is parallel on
Y
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose that
df
is a parallel 1-form on
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
. Then
f_{0}:Y\to S^{1}
defines a smooth fiber bundle
F-Y\xrightarrow{f_{0}}S^{1}
, and the infinite cyclic covering space is a cylinder
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}=\mathbb{R}\times F
.

Proof.

Differentiating the pullback diagram yields

d(\exp)\circ df=df_{0}\circ dp.

The fact that
df
is parallel (and nonzero) implies that
df
is a nonvanishing 1-form, hence
f
is a smooth submersion. Since the covering maps
\exp
and
p
are local diffeomorphisms,
f_{0}
is also a smooth submersion. Moreover,
f_{0}
is surjective because its image
f_{0}(Y)
is both open and closed in
S^{1}
, and
f_{0}
is a proper map because
Y
is compact. By Ehresmann’s fibration theorem
[
Voi02
, Theorem 9.3]
it follows that
f_{0}:Y\to S^{1}
is a smooth locally trivial fibration, which in this case implies that it is in fact a smooth fiber bundle, because
Y
and
S^{1}
are smooth compact manifolds.

Now the infinite cyclic covering space is by definition the pullback of the fiber bundle
Y\to S^{1}
along
\exp:\mathbb{R}\to S^{1}
, so
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
is a smooth fiber bundle over
\mathbb{R}
. But
\mathbb{R}
is contractible so
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}
is a trivial fiber bundle over
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

As a result, if the end-periodic fiber bundle has
m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)\equiv 0
, then the fibers of the infinite cyclic covering
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}\to B
are all cylinders and thus the periodic end is actually a cylindrical end. Combining this with Proposition
5.4
, we have shown that the end-periodic fiber bundle is cylindrical if and only if the Hessian
m_{0}(\nabla^{T^{*}Y}df)
vanishes identically.

6.  Chern character of the index bundle

In this section we will integrate our transgression formula (
5.6
) to obtain a local index formula in the end-periodic family setting, following the approach of
[
BGV92
, §9.3]
and
[
MP97
, §15]
.

Integrating the transgression formula (
5.6
) yields

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}

\displaystyle\rch(A_{t})-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\rch(A_{t})=

(6.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}({\dagger})-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}({\dagger})-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)\,dt-d_{B}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\right)d\xi\,dt\right)

where we have denoted

({\dagger})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

We will show that the long-time and short-time limits of the renormalized Chern character converge to

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\rch(A_{t})-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\rch(A_{t})=\ch(\nabla_{0})-\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z)).

On the right-hand side of (
6.1
), we will show that the limits converge to

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}({\dagger})-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}({\dagger})=0-\int_{W_{0}/B}f\,\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Y)).

We define the end-periodic eta form as the integral over
t\in(0,\infty)
of
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
,

\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)\,dt.

Finally, the remaining exact term in the transgression formula, integrated over
t\in(0,\infty)
, is obviously exact.

First we verify that the integral over
t\in(0,\infty)
of
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
converges to a well-defined differential form on
B
. Our proof is similar to the analogous fact
[
MP97
, Proposition 12]
in the context of manifolds with boundary.

Proposition 6.1
.

The integral over
t\in(0,\infty)
of the time-dependent end-periodic eta form
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)
converges in
\Omega^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(B)
, so we have a well-defined differential form

\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)\,dt\in\Omega^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(B).

Proof.

The time-dependent end-periodic eta form is defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(d_{Y/B}f)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(Y)(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-\frac{1}{\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(Y)(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

We examine the rate of decay/growth in
t
for both of these integrands. Looking at the first term here, since
D^{z}(Y)(\xi)
has trivial nullspace for each
z\in B
and
\xi\in S^{1}
, we have

|e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}|\leq Ce^{-\lambda t}

where
\lambda>0
is the infimum of the smallest eigenvalues of
D^{z}(Y)(\xi)^{2}
over
z\in B
, cf.
[
BGV92
, Lemma 9.5]
. Moreover
\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(Y)(\xi)
decays like
t^{-1/2}
which simply balances with the extra
t^{1/2}
coefficient, hence

t^{1/2}\Str\left(\cl(d_{Y/B}f)\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(Y)(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)

decays exponentially as
t\to\infty
and is therefore integrable as
t\to\infty
. On the other hand, as
t\to 0^{+}
we have

\Str\left(\accentset{\mbox{\large.}}{A}_{t}(Y)(\xi)e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}\right)\leq O(t^{-1/2})

by
[
BGV92
, Theorem 9.23]
, so the integrand in the first term is also integrable as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Now looking at the second term

\gamma_{2}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(Y)(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)\right)\frac{d\xi}{\xi}

a direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle[A_{t}(Y)(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]

\displaystyle=t[D(Y)(\xi),\cl(d_{Y/B}f)]+t^{1/2}[D(Y)(\xi),\pi^{*}d_{B}f]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt+t^{1/2}[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\cl(d_{Y/B}f)]+[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\pi^{*}d_{B}f]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt+[A_{[2]}(Y),cl(d_{Y/B}f)]+t^{-1/2}[A_{[2]}(Y),\pi^{*}d_{B}f].

and in fact
[A_{[2]}(Y),\pi^{*}d_{B}f]=0
, which annihilates the term which is most problematic as
t\to 0^{+}
. To see this, one may take a local orthonormal frame
(e_{j})
for
T(Y/B)
and
(e_{\alpha})
for
\pi^{*}TB
, and any
\omega\otimes u\in\Gamma(M;\mathbb{E}_{Y})=\Gamma(M;\pi^{*}\Lambda T^{*}B\otimes E_{Y})
, and calculate

\displaystyle(A_{[2]}(Y)

\displaystyle\cdot\pi^{*}d_{B}f)(\omega\otimes u)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{4}\sum_{\alpha<\beta}\sum_{j}e^{\alpha}\wedge e^{\beta}\cl(e^{j})\left(\Omega_{Y}(e_{\alpha},e_{\beta})(e_{j})(\pi^{*}d_{B}f\wedge\omega\otimes u)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{4}\sum_{\alpha<\beta}\sum_{j}\bigg(e^{\alpha}\wedge e^{\beta}\,\Omega_{Y}(e_{\alpha},e_{\beta})(e_{j})

\displaystyle\hskip 79.66771pt\cdot(-1)^{\deg(u)+1}(\pi^{*}d_{B}f\wedge\omega)\otimes\cl(e^{j})(u)\bigg)

and on the other hand, since
\pi^{*}d_{B}f
can be commuted past the 2-form
e^{\alpha}\wedge e^{\beta}
,

\displaystyle(\pi^{*}d_{B}f\cdot A_{[2]}(Y))(\omega\otimes u)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{4}\sum_{\alpha<\beta}\sum_{j}\bigg(\pi^{*}d_{B}f\wedge e^{\alpha}\wedge e^{\beta}\,\Omega_{Y}(e_{\alpha},e_{\beta})(e_{j})

\displaystyle\hskip 79.66771pt\cdot(-1)^{\deg(u)}\omega\otimes\cl(e^{j})(u)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{4}\sum_{\alpha<\beta}\sum_{j}\bigg(e^{\alpha}\wedge e^{\beta}\,\Omega_{Y}(e_{\alpha},e_{\beta})(e_{j})

\displaystyle\hskip 79.66771pt\cdot(-1)^{\deg(u)}(\pi^{*}d_{B}f\wedge\omega)\otimes\cl(e^{j})(u)\bigg)

\displaystyle=-(A_{[2]}(Y)\cdot\pi^{*}d_{B}f)(\omega\otimes u),

hence
[A_{2}(Y),\pi^{*}d_{B}f]=A_{2}(Y)(\pi^{*}d_{B}f)+(\pi^{*}d_{B}f)A_{[2]}(Y)=0
. Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}[A_{t}(Y)(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]

\displaystyle=[D(Y)(\xi),\cl(d_{Y/B}f)]+\frac{1}{2}t^{-1/2}[D(Y)(\xi),\pi^{*}d_{B}f]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt+\frac{1}{2}t^{-1/2}[A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi),\cl(d_{Y/B}f)].

As
t\to\infty
this operator does not slow down the overall rate of convergence of the integrand, and so integrability as
t\to\infty
follows from the same argument we made before for the other integrand, namely the rapid decay of the heat operator
\mathcal{H}_{t}(\xi)
as
t\to\infty
.

As for integrability when
t\to 0^{+}
, note that the operator
\partial_{t}[A_{t}(Y)(\xi),\delta_{t}m_{0}(df)]
grows at worst like
t^{-1/2}
as
t\to 0^{+}
. By Proposition
3.3
, the indicial family
A(Y)(\xi)
can be twisted by a flat connection to produce a Bismut superconnection
\mathbb{A}
on a twisted bundle
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}
. The local heat invariants of
\mathbb{A}
and
A(Y)(\xi)
coincide exactly because
\mathbb{A}
is obtained from
A(Y)(\xi)
by twisting with a flat connection on a line bundle over
Y
, hence the coefficients of the short-time asymptotic expansion of the heat kernel for
A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)
coincide with those of
\mathbb{A}_{t}^{2}
. By
[
BGV92
, Theorem 10.21]
the lowest order term (in
t
) in the short-time asymptotic expansion for the latter heat kernel is
t^{0}
. Thus, overall, the integrand in
\gamma_{2}(t)
grows at worst like
t^{-1/2}
as
t\to 0^{+}
, which is integrable as
t\to 0^{+}
. Since both integrands are integrable as
t\to\infty
and
t\to 0^{+}
, the integral over
t\in(0,\infty)
converges in
\Omega^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(B)
.
∎

Before we state the index formula, we briefly recall the definition of the
K
-theoretic index bundle and its Chern character in de Rham cohomology. It is convenient to consider two cases:

1.

The
constant rank
case: where
\dim\ker(D^{z})^{+}
is constant over
z\in B
, in which case the spaces
\ker(D^{z})^{+}
and
\ker(D^{z})^{-}
assemble vector bundles and hence
\Ker D=\Ker D^{+}\oplus\Ker D^{-}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded vector bundle.

2.

The
non-constant rank
case: where
\dim\ker(D^{z})^{+}
is allowed to vary over
z\in B
, hence the spaces
\ker(D^{z})^{+}
and
\ker(D^{z})^{-}
do not assemble vector bundles.

Recall that the
K
-theory group
K(B)
is by definition the Grothendieck group generated by isomorphism classes of vector bundles over
B
. In the constant-rank case the index bundle is simply the
K
-theory element

\Ind D=[\Ker D^{+}]-[\Ker D^{-}]\in K(B)

and the Chern character of the index bundle is the cohomology class

\ch(\Ind D)=[\ch(\nabla_{0})]=[\Str(e^{-\nabla_{0}^{2}})]\in H_{dR}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(B)

where
\nabla_{0}=P_{0}A_{[1]}P_{0}
is the induced connection on
\Ker D
, with
P_{0}
the projection onto the kernel. The cohomology class is independent of the choice of connection on the kernel.

In the non-constant rank case the kernels
\ker D^{z}
no longer assemble a vector bundle and we need another way to define the index bundle. In this case we follow the standard stabilization procedure, cf.
[
MP97
, §6]
and
[
BGV92
, §9.5]
. The stabilization procedure constructs a vertical family of odd smoothing operators
P\in\Psi^{-\infty}_{\pi}(M;\mathbb{E})
so that the perturbed operator
D+P
is injective on each fiber, hence
\Coker(D+P)
is a well-defined vector bundle. Then the index bundle of
D
is defined as the
K
-theory class

\Ind D=[B\times\mathbb{C}^{N}]-[\Coker(D+P)]\in K(B)

(6.2)

where
N
is a positive integer such that

\Gamma(M_{z};E_{z}^{+})/V_{z}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}

for every
z\in B
, with a subspace
V_{z}\subseteq\Gamma(M_{z};E_{z}^{+})
constructed in such a way as to essentially fulfill the role of
(\ker(D^{z})^{+})^{\perp}
, but yielding a dimension
N
which is independent of
z
.

Write
\Ind D=[V^{+}]-[V^{-}]
and think of
V^{+}
and
V^{-}
as the even and odd components of a superbundle
V=V^{+}\oplus V^{-}\to B
. Then the Chern character of
\Ind D
is defined as the cohomology class

\ch(\Ind D)=\ch(V^{+})-\ch(V^{-})=[\ch(A^{+})]-[\ch(A^{-})]\in H_{dR}^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}}(B)

where
A
is any superconnection on
V
, with chiral parts
A^{+}
and
A^{-}
. With this setup, our index formula can be stated as follows.

Theorem 6.2

(Index formula)
.

Let
M\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle and let
E\to M
be a family of end-periodic Clifford modules, with
D
the associated family of end-periodic Dirac operators, and suppose that the family
D
is Fredholm. Then
\ch(\Ind D)\in H_{dR}^{\mathrm{ev}}(B)
is represented in de Rham cohomology by

\displaystyle\ch(\nabla_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))-\int_{W_{0}/B}f\,\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Y))-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt-d_{B}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\Str\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}}{\partial\xi}\right)d\xi\,dt\right).

Moreover, the degree zero part of the end-periodic eta form
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}
is the fiberwise end-periodic eta invariant, i.e.,
(\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}})_{[0]}\in C^{\infty}(B)
given by
z\mapsto\eta_{\mathrm{ep}}(D^{z}(Y_{z}))
.

The statement about the degree zero component of the end-periodic eta form follows from equation (
5.8
). The proof Theorem
6.2
will be completed by calculating the limits in the integrated transgression formula (
6.1
), which we will do in §
6.1
–
6.4
below. Throughout §
6.1
–
6.3
we assume that the family of Dirac operators
D
has constant rank nullspace over
z\in B
. In §
6.4
we show that the general case reduces to this case, and that the index formula holds as stated without any constant rank assumption.

6.1. Short-time limit of the renormalized Chern character

We will calculate the limit of
\rch(A_{t})
as
t\to 0^{+}
using the short-time asymptotic expansion for the heat kernel. The relevant short-time expansions for us are given by
[
BGV92
, Theorem 10.21]
(for a closed manifold, or a family of closed manifolds) and
[
Roe88
, Proposition 2.11]
(for a manifold of bounded geometry). Combining these two results gives us the short-time asymptotic expansion for a family of heat kernels associated with the end-periodic Bismut superconnection.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
M^{n}\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle and let
E\to M
be a family of end-periodic Clifford modules, with
D=D(M)
the associated family of end-periodic Dirac operators. Let
A
denote the end-periodic Bismut superconnection adapted to
D
, and let
K_{t}=(K_{t}^{z})_{z\in B}
denote the associated family of heat kernels, i.e., the family of integral kernels for the heat operator
e^{-A_{t}^{2}}
. Then we have an asymptotic expansion as
t\to 0^{+}
,

K_{t}(x,x)\sim(4\pi t)^{-n/2}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}t^{i}k_{i}(x)

with coefficients
k_{i}\in\sum_{j\leq 2i}\Lambda^{j}T^{*}M\otimes\End_{\Cl(T^{*}(M/B))}(E)
, such that

\sum_{i=0}^{n/2}(k_{i})_{[2i]}=\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(M)).

Using the short-time asymptotic expansion we will show that

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\rch(A_{t})=\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))

where
Z
is the compact part of the end-periodic fiber bundle. Consider the definition of the renormalized Chern character

\displaystyle\rch(A_{t})

\displaystyle=\rStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(\int_{Z_{N}/B}\str(K_{t}(x,x))\,dx-(N+1)\int_{W_{0}/B}\str(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}_{t}(x,x))\,dx\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}s_{N}(t).

For each fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
we can deduce from Proposition
6.3
that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}s_{N}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\left(\int_{Z_{N}/B}\str(K_{t}(x,x))\,dx-(N+1)\int_{W_{0}/B}\str(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{K}}_{t}(x,x))\,dx\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{Z_{N}/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(M))\,dx-(N+1)\int_{W_{0}/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}))\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))\,dx.

By Proposition
4.17
the limit
\lim_{N\to\infty}s_{N}(t)
converges uniformly on bounded time intervals, and so we are justified in interchanging limits to calculate

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\rch(A_{t})

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\lim_{N\to\infty}s_{N}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}s_{N}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z)).

6.2. Long-time limit of the renormalized Chern character

Let
M\to B
be an end-periodic fiber bundle and
E\to M
a family of end-periodic Clifford modules, with associated family of end-periodic Dirac operators
D=(D^{z})_{z_{\in B}}
and end-periodic Bismut superconnection
A
adapted to
D
. We let
P_{0}
denote the projection onto
\Ker D
, and
\nabla_{0}=\nabla^{\Ker D}=P_{0}A_{[1]}P_{0}
the connection on
\Ker D
induced by
A
. We recall that we are operating under the following assumptions:

1.

The operators
(D^{z})_{z\in B}
are Fredholm on
H^{1}(M_{z};E_{z})
, which is equivalent to saying that the normal operators
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{z}
are invertible for every
z\in B
or that the indicial families
D(Y)^{z}(\xi)
are invertible for every
z\in B
and
\xi\in S^{1}
.

2.

The family
D=(D^{z})_{z\in B}
has nullspace of constant rank over
z\in B
.

3.

The base
B
is compact.

Under these assumptions, the arguments of
[
BGV92
, Proposition 9.20, Lemma 9.20, Lemma 9.21]
go through with little change, using the properties of the heat kernel on a smooth Riemannian manifold with bounded geometry, cf.
[
Roe88
]
. By these arguments the heat operator
e^{-A_{t}^{2}}
can be understood by diagonalizing and then exponentiating. Let
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
denote the projections onto
\Ker D
and
\Rg D
. We can find
g
such that

A^{2}=g^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}U&0\\
0&V\end{bmatrix}g

with
U=\nabla_{0}^{2}+U^{+},
and
V=P_{1}AP_{1}+V^{+}
. Rescaling in
t>0
we get

\delta_{t}(A^{2})=\delta_{t}(g)^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\delta_{t}(U)&0\\
0&\delta_{t}(V)\end{bmatrix}\delta_{t}(g)

which then yields

e^{-A_{t}^{2}}=e^{-t\delta_{t}(A^{2})}=\delta_{t}(g)^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}e^{-t\delta_{t}(U)}&0\\
0&e^{-t\delta_{t}(V)}\end{bmatrix}\delta_{t}(g),

from which we deduce

e^{-t\delta_{t}U}=e^{-\nabla_{0}^{2}}+O(t^{-1/2}).

Using
[
BGV92
, Lemma 9.21]
to handle the term
e^{-t\delta_{t}(V)}
, we choose a precompact open subset
S\subseteq B
and for any
z\in S
set
\lambda^{z}=
smallest nonzero eigenvalue of
(D^{z})^{2}
. Then for
\lambda=\inf_{z\in S}\lambda^{z}
we have

P_{1}e^{-t\delta_{t}V}P_{1}=O(e^{-t\lambda/2})

uniformly on precompact open subsets of
B
. These estimates, together with the Volterra series for the heat kernels of
U
and
V
, cf.
[
MP97
, equation (11.2)]
, imply that

\rStr(e^{-A_{t}^{2}})=\rStr(e^{-\nabla_{0}^{2}})+O(t^{-1/2}).

Note that
e^{-\nabla_{0}^{2}}
is trace-class by the invertibilty of the normal operators
\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{D}}^{z}
for every
z\in B
, hence the renormalization factor vanishes and
\rStr(e^{-\nabla_{0}^{2}})=\Str(e^{-\nabla_{0}^{2}})
. Thus taking the limit as
t\to\infty
yields

\lim_{t\to\infty}\rch(A_{t})=\ch(\nabla_{0}).

(6.3)

6.3. Limits on the right-hand side

In this section we will calculate the long-time and short-time limits of the term
({\dagger})
in (
6.1
) by applying the framework of §
3
, where we showed that the indicial family
A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)
when twisted by a flat connection produces a Bismut superconnection on a twisted bundle.

Proposition 6.4
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
6.2
, we define

({\dagger})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}.

Then we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}({\dagger})=0\mbox{ \ and \ }\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}({\dagger})=\int_{W_{0}/B}f\cdot\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(Y/B).

Proof.

For the long-time limit, by applying
[
BGV92
, Theorem 9.19]
we calculate

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\str\left(K_{\exp(-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi))}(x,x)\right)=\str\left(K_{\exp(\nabla_{0}^{2})}(x,x)\right)=0

where
\nabla_{0}=P_{0}A_{[1]}(Y)(\xi)P_{0}
is the induced connection on
\Ker D_{\xi}(Y)
, which is just the zero space under the assumption that
D=D(M)
is Fredholm. Therefore
\lim_{t\to\infty}({\dagger})=0
.

For the short-time limit, consider the Bismut superconnection
\mathbb{A}
on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}
defined in Proposition
3.3
as the twist of
A(Y)(\xi)
by the flat connection
d
on the bundle
L_{\xi}
. Since
\mathbb{A}
is obtained by twisting
A(Y)(\xi)
by this flat connection on a rank-1 bundle, the local heat invariants in the short-time asymptotic expansion for the heat operators
e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}
and
e^{-\mathbb{A}_{t}^{2}}
coincide exactly. Therefore, applying
[
BGV92
, Theorem 10.23]
to the rescaled Bismut superconnection
\mathbb{A}_{t}
on
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\otimes L_{\xi}
we calculate

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\str\left(K_{\exp(-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi))}(x,x)\right)=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\str\left(K_{e^{-{\mathbb{A}_{t}}}}(x,x)\right)=\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(Y/B)\ch(L_{\xi})=\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(Y/B)

since the Chern character is multiplicative over tensor products, and
L_{\xi}
is equipped with a flat connection which implies
\ch(L_{\xi})=1
. Therefore

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}({\dagger})=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|\xi|=1}\int_{W_{0}/B}f(x)\str\left(K_{e^{-A_{t}^{2}(Y)(\xi)}}(x,x)\right)dx\frac{d\xi}{\xi}=\int_{W_{0}/B}f\cdot\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(Y/B).

∎

6.4. Non-constant rank case

Now we remove the requirement that the family of Dirac operators
D=(D^{z})_{z\in B}
have constant
\dim\ker(D^{z})^{+}
over
z\in B
. In this case, the kernels
\ker D^{z}
no longer assemble a vector bundle, and the standard stabilization procedure perturbs
D
by a vertical family of odd smoothing operators
P\in\Psi^{-\infty}_{\pi}(M;\mathbb{E})
so that
D+P
is injective on each fiber, hence
\Coker(D+P)\to B
is a well-defined vector bundle, and the index bundle
\Ind D\in K(B)
is defined as in equation (
6.2
).

Let
A^{P}
denote the Bismut superconnection adapted to the perturbed family
D+P
and let
A_{t}^{P}
denote the rescaled superconnection. Then we know from the constant-rank case that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\rch(A_{t}^{P})=\ch(\nabla^{\Ker(D+P)})

(6.4)

cf.
[
BGV92
, Theorem 9.26]
, and it is straightforward to calculate, using the same methods as before, that

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\rch(A_{t}^{P})=\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))+\ch(B\times\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(6.5)

Then taking the transgression formula with the perturbed Bismut superconnection
A_{t}^{P}
and integrating over
t\in(0,\infty)
yields

\lim_{t\to\infty}\rch(A_{t}^{P})-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\rch(A_{t}^{P})=\lim_{t\to\infty}({\dagger}^{P})-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}({\dagger}^{P})-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}^{P}

where the superscript
P
on the right-hand side indicates that the correction term involves the perturbed operator
A_{t}^{P}
in its definition instead of
A_{t}
. Now from the definition of the index bundle we have

\Ind(D)=[\Ker(D+P)]-[B\times\mathbb{C}^{N}]

and thus, using (
6.4
) and (
6.5
),

\displaystyle\ch(\Ind D)

\displaystyle=[\ch(\nabla^{\Ker(D+P)})]_{\mathrm{dR}}-N

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}[\rch(A_{t}^{P})]_{\mathrm{dR}}-N

\displaystyle=\left[\int_{Z/B}\mathrm{\mathbf{I}}(D(Z))+\lim_{t\to\infty}({\dagger}^{P})-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}({\dagger}^{P})-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}^{P}\right]_{\mathrm{dR}}

where two factors of
N
have canceled each other, and the notation
[\ \cdot\ ]_{\mathrm{dR}}
indicates a de Rham cohomology class. Thus the index formula will follow from this equation as soon as we calculate these correction terms associated with the perturbed superconnection
A_{t}^{P}
.

In fact, these correction terms depend only upon the indicial family of the superconnection
A_{t}
. We will show that, for a careful choice of perturbation
P
, the indicial family is unchanged by the perturbation, and so the limits
\lim_{t\to\infty}({\dagger}^{P})
and
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}({\dagger}^{P})
are exactly the same as the limits we calculated in §
6.3
. Similarly, the end-periodic eta form is unaffected by the perturbation, that is
\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}^{P}=\widehat{\eta}_{\mathrm{ep}}
.

Following this idea, we aim to construct a family of rank-stabilizing perturbations
P=(P^{z})_{z\in B}
so that (i)
D+P
is a family having nullspace of constant rank, and (ii)
N(P)=\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{P}}=0
(in particular,
P
will be trace-class). Since the correction terms in the index formula depend only on the normal operator (or indicial family), the correction terms are unchanged when we replace
D
with
D+P
. This will complete the proof of the index formula in the non-constant rank case.

We will adapt the proof of
[
MR05
, Lemma 1.1]
to our context. One of the key components is the parametrix construction for the Dirac operator. Under the assumption that
D
is Fredholm (i.e., the normal operator
N(D)
is invertible), there exists a parametrix
G
such that
GD=I-R
where the error
R
is a compact operator. We additionally want to show that we can construct the parametrix so that the error is a compact
smoothing
operator. In order to establish this we will make use of the uniform calculus of pseudodifferential operators
\mathcal{U}\Psi^{s}(M;E)
, cf.
[
Shu92
, Definition 3.2]
. The uniform calculus can be defined for any manifold of bounded geometry, in particular for an end-periodic manifold.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
M
be an end-periodic manifold and let
D
be an end-periodic Dirac operator on
C_{c}^{\infty}(M;E)
. Suppose that the normal operator
N(D)
is invertible. Then
D
has a parametrix
G\in\mathcal{U}\Psi^{-1}(M;E)
such that the error
R=I-GD
is a compact smoothing operator.

Proof.

First we will construct a parametrix for
D
with compact error following the method of
[
Maz91
, Lemma 5.5]
. Let
Z_{3}=Z\sqcup W_{0}\sqcup W_{1}\sqcup W_{2}
and let
\widehat{Z}_{3}
denote the double of
Z_{3}
across the boundary. Let
D(\widehat{Z}_{3})
denote the Dirac operator acting on sections of
E|_{\widehat{Z}_{3}}
, with respect to the obvious geometric data. Since
\widehat{Z}_{3}
is a closed manifold,
D(\widehat{Z}_{3})
is Fredholm, so we can find a parametrix
A
such that
C=I-AD(\widehat{Z}_{3})
is compact. Now by patching together the parametrix
A
for
D(\widehat{Z}_{3})
near
Z
, with the inverse normal operator
N(D)^{-1}
along the end, we define an operator

G_{1}=\psi_{1}A\phi_{1}+\psi_{2}N(D)^{-1}\phi_{2}.

The smooth cutoffs
\psi_{j},\phi_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
are defined so that:

•

\phi_{1}|_{Z\sqcup W_{0}}=1
and
\phi_{1}|_{M\setminus Z_{2}}=0
,

•

\phi_{2}=1-\phi_{1}
,

•

\supp(\psi_{1})\subset Z_{3}
and
\psi_{1}|_{\supp(\phi_{1})}=1
,

•

\supp(\psi_{2})\subset\mathchoice{\accentset{\displaystyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\textstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-5.59721pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-3.91806pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}{\accentset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\smash{\raisebox{-2.7986pt}{$\widetildesym$}}}}{Y}}_{+}
and
\psi_{2}|_{\supp(\phi_{2})}=1
.

Thus the segment
W_{2}
serves as the “patching region”. A direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle G_{1}D

\displaystyle=[D,\psi_{1}]A\phi_{1}+\psi_{1}AD\phi_{1}+[D,\psi_{2}]N(D)^{-1}\phi_{2}+\psi_{2}DN(D)^{-1}\phi_{2}

\displaystyle=[D,\psi_{1}]A\phi_{1}+[D,\psi_{2}]N(D)^{-1}\phi_{2}-\psi_{1}C\phi_{1}+(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2})

\displaystyle=I-(\mathrm{compact\,operator}).

In the last line one can argue exactly as in
[
Maz91
, Lemma 5.5]
that the error is compact using the compact inclusion afforded by Rellich’s lemma and the fact that the terms are smoothing of order at least
-1
. Thus we have a parametrix
G_{1}
for
D
which is a (classical) pseudodifferential operator of order
-1
, such that the error
R_{1}=I-G_{1}D
is compact.

Next we will show that
G_{1}\in\mathcal{U}\Psi^{-1}(M;E)
is a uniform pseudodifferential operator, cf.
[
Shu92
, Definition 3.2]
. From the patching construction we have
G_{1}=\psi_{1}A\phi_{1}+\psi_{2}N(D)^{-1}\phi_{2}
, and it is clear that
\psi_{1}A\phi_{1}
is uniform because
A
is a parametrix for a Fredholm operator on the closed manifold
\widehat{Z}_{3}
, so it will suffice to show that
